q ​ t ​ RSK ∗ qt{\rm RSK}^{*} : A probabilistic dual RSK correspondence for Macdonald polynomials
Gabriel Frieden, Florian Schreier-Aigner

Abstract
. We introduce a probabilistic generalization of the dualRobinson–Schensted–Knuth correspondence, called q ​ t ​ RSK ∗ qt{\rm RSK}^{*} , depending on two parameters q q and t t . This correspondence extends the q ​ RS ​ t q{\rm RS}t correspondence, recently introduced by the authors, and allows the first tableaux-theoretic proof of the dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials. By specializing q q and t t , one recovers the row and column insertion version of the classical dual RSK correspondence as well as of q q - and t t -deformations thereof which are connected to q q -Whittaker and Hall–Littlewood polynomials. When restricting to Jack polynomials and { 0 , 1 } \{0,1\} -matrices corresponding to words, we prove that the insertion tableaux obtained by q ​ t ​ RSK ∗ qt{\rm RSK}^{*} are invariant under swapping letters in the input word. Our approach is based on Fomin’s growth diagrams and the notion of probabilistic bijections.



1.
Introduction

The Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK) correspondence is a bijection between matrices of nonnegative integers with finite support and pairs of semistandard Young tableaux of the same shape. It was introduced by Knuth
[
11
]
and generalizes the Robinson-Schensted (RS) correspondence which was introduced by Robinson
[
17
]
for permutations and independently by Schensted
[
18
]
for words
1
1

1

We refer by “RS” to the special case of RSK for permutations and by “RS for words” to the special case of RSK for words.
. Both correspondences have many applications in combinatorics, representation theory, probability theory and algebraic geometry. In particular RSK gives a bijective proof of the Cauchy identity

(1)

\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})=\prod_{i,j\geq 1}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}},

where the sum is over all partitions
\lambda
and
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z})
denotes the Schur function in the variables
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots)
. A closely related bijection is the dual RSK correspondence (
{\rm RSK}^{*}
) introduced by Knuth
[
11
]
which yields a bijective proof of the dual Cauchy identity

(2)

\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})=\prod_{i,j\geq 1}(1+x_{i}y_{j}),

where the sum is again over all partitions
\lambda
.

All of the above mentioned correspondences have been extended into various directions throughout the last decades. Among others several randomized generalizations of RS, RSK and
{\rm RSK}^{*}
were introduced in

[
2
,
3
,
4
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. These generalizations associate to each permutation or nonnegative integer matrix respectively a distribution on pairs of (semi)standard Young tableaux depending on a parameter
q
or
t
and thereby giving a proof of the (dual) Cauchy identity for
q
-Whittaker or Hall–Littlewood symmetric functions. Similar to the classical RSK algorithm, these randomized generalizations have many applications to probabilistic models, compare for example with
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
13
]
.

In a previous paper
[
1
]
, the authors introduced a randomized generalization for RS called
q{\rm RS}t
depending on two parameter
q
and
t
. This generalization was designed to prove the squarefree part of the Cauchy identity for the Macdonald symmetric functions
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
and
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
. Analogously to Macdonald symmetric functions, which specialize to
q
-Whittaker, Hall–Littlewood and Schur symmetric functions, the
q{\rm RS}t
correspondence specializes to the corresponding randomized variations of RS for
q=0
,
t=0
,
q\rightarrow\infty
or
t\rightarrow\infty
, and to the row- or column-insertion of RS itself for
q=t=0
or
q=t\rightarrow\infty
respectively.

The aim of this paper is to give a unifying generalization of both
q{\rm RS}t
and
{\rm RSK}^{*}
, called
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
, and thereby giving the first tableaux theoretic proof of the dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials

(3)

\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q)=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

Although
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
specializes to the dual row-insertion and dual column-insertion it is not a simple generalization of both algorithms but a “superposition”. In particular the insertion of a sequence of integers
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{r}
is done in ascending order for dual column-insertion, in descending order for dual row-insertion, and so-to-speak simultaneously.

1.1.
Bijectivizing an algebraic proof

A very concise proof of the classical (dual) Cauchy identity for Schur symmetric functions is based on the commutation relation of the up and (dual) down operators on Young’s lattice. The framework of Fomin’s growth diagrams
[
9
,
8
]
allows one to translate a bijective proof of the commutation relation easily into a bijective proof of the (dual) Cauchy identity.
By defining the Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
through their monomial expansion

(4)

P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)}\psi_{T}(q,t)\textbf{x}^{T},

where
\psi_{T}(q,t)
is a rational function on
q
and
t
,
the (dual) Cauchy identity is again an immediate consequence of the commutation relation

(5)

D^{*}_{y}(q,t)U_{x}(q,t)=(1+xy)U_{x}(q,t)D^{*}_{y}(q,t),

where
U_{x}(q,t)
and
D^{*}_{y}(q,t)
are the
(q,t)
-weighted extensions of the up and dual down operators on Young’s lattice.
By restricting the above to the coefficient of
xy
, we obtain the commutation relation

D_{q,t}U_{q,t}=\frac{1-t}{1-q}{\rm id}+U_{q,t}D_{q,t}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad D_{q,t}U_{q,t}-U_{q,t}D_{q,t}=\frac{1-t}{1-q}{\rm id},

which was the fundamental commutation relation in the authors previous paper
[
1
]
. By further setting
q=t=0
the above becomes the equation

DU-UD={\rm id},

which is the defining property for Young’s lattice to be a differential poset, see
[
19
]
.

It is clear that no bijective proof of the commutation relation (
5
) which preserves the
(q,t)
-weight can exist since it would imply a weight-preserving bijective proof of the dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials. However, as we see in Table
1
this is not possible. Our approach is nevertheless to prove (
5
) “as bijectively as possible” by using the notion of
probabilistic bijections
introduced in
[
3
,
5
]
.
Let
X,Y
be sets together with weight functions
\omega
and
\overline{\omega}
respectively which satisfy

(6)

\sum_{x\in X}\omega(x)=\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\omega}(y).

Instead of one weight preserving bijection between
X
and
Y
, a probabilistic bijection consists of two families of ‘‘probability distributions’’
2
2

2

Note that the term “probability distribution” is used in a more general setting allowing them to have values in
\mathbb{Q}(q,t)
instead of real values in
[0,1]
; nevertheless the sum of the entries of the “probability distribution” has to be
1
.
. For each
x\in X
we have a “forward” probability distribution
\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)
, which can be thought of as the probability of mapping
x
to
y
, and for each
y\in Y
a “backward” probability distribution
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)
, the probability of mapping
y
back to
x
. Probability distributions satisfying the equation

(7)

\omega(x)\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)\overline{\omega}(y),

are called
probabilistic bijections
. It is immediate, however very significant, that the above compatibility condition between the probabilistic distributions and the weights implies (
6
).

Our combinatorial proof of the commutation relation (
5
) boils down to finding, for partitions
\lambda,\rho
, a probabilistic bijection between two sets
\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)
,
\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)
of partitions together with weights coming from the definition of the
(q,t)
-up and
(q,t)
-dual down operator.
The main guiding principle for finding the explicit formulas for both the forward and backward probabilities

\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)
in this setting was to use (
7
) as a defining relation and to distribute the factors in
\frac{\omega(x)}{\omega(y)}
evenly in a minimal way such that both
\mathcal{P}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
are homogeneous rational functions in certain parameters. The actual definition of the probabilities however is based on an intuitive geometric approach using the
Quebecois notation
for Young diagrams, a variation of both the French and English notation,
for which it takes a decent amount of work to prove (
7
).
On the other hand, our definition of the probabilities is not only very concise, but also naturally led us to rediscovering an extension of Lagrange interpolation for symmetric polynomials by Chen and Louck
[
6
]
which immediately implies that the forward and backward probabilities are indeed probability distributions.

Using Fomin’s growth diagrams allows us to lift our probabilistic bijection of the commutation relation to a probabilistic bijection of the dual Cauchy identity. The next table shows the forward probabilities for matrices and pairs of tableaux corresponding to the coefficient of
x_{1}^{2}x_{2}y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}
in (
3
). We can observe easily in this example that
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
is a probability distribution since the sum over the probabilities in each row yields
1
.

Table 1
.

The forward probabilities
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
where all matrices have weight
x_{1}^{2}x_{2}y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}
and the weight of the pair of tableaux are
x_{1}^{2}x_{2}y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}
times the according entry in the last row.

1.2.
Properties of the
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence

Our randomized
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence can be specialized in two different ways: one can specialize the parameters
q,t
or restrict the correspondence to a smaller family of matrices.

For
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
the forward and backward probabilities of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
become actual probabilities, i.e., they have values in
[0,1]
.
By setting
t=0
or
t\rightarrow\infty
we obtain the
q
-Whittaker dual row insertion
or
q
-Whittaker dual column insertion
respectively for
q
-Whittaker polynomials which were first defined by Matveev and Petrov
[
13
, §5.1, §5.4]
. By a symmetry property of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
these two specializations are connected to setting
q=0
or
q\rightarrow\infty
, i.e.,
t
-deformations of the row and column version of
{\rm RSK}^{*}
for Hall-Littlewood polynomials. Finally for
q=t=0
or
q=t\rightarrow\infty
we obtain the row or column insertion version of
{\rm RSK}^{*}
; this can be checked easily in the example presented in Table
1
.

Another interesting specialization of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
comes from setting
q=t^{\alpha}
and taking the limit
t\rightarrow 1
; this corresponds to specializing Macdonald polynomials to Jack polynomials. By restricting to matrices with at most one nonzero entry in each column, the insertion tableau obtained by the
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence is invariant with respect to interchanging columns of the input matrix. In Table
2
this can be observed by the fact that the sum of the probabilities in the first two columns as well as the probability in last column are equal for all three input matrices.

The forward probabilities

in the Jack limit.

By restricting the input of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
to
\{0,1\}
-matrices with at most one entry equal to
1
in each column we obtain a
(q,t)
-deformation of RS for words. By further restricting to permutation matrices we obtain
q{\rm RS}t
. These specializations are summarized in the next table.

\begin{array}[]{lp{2.7cm}l|p{2.5cm}}\text{Corresp.}&\text{Domain&\text{Image}&\text{Macdonald case\\
\hline\cr\text{RS}&$\pi\in S_{n}$&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\lambda\vdash n}{\rm SYT}_{\lambda}\times{\rm SYT}_{\lambda}&\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{AignerFrieden22}{}{}, Def 4.6.5]}\\[18.0pt]
\text{RS for words}&$w\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{*}$&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\lambda}{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}\times{\rm SYT}_{\lambda}&\text{Definition~\ref{def:qrst insertion}\\[18.0pt]
{\rm RSK}^{*}&$\{0,1\}$\text{-matrices&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\lambda}{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}\times{\rm SSYT}^{*}_{\lambda}&\text{ Theorem~\ref{thm:dRSK is prob bij},} \text{Definition~\ref{def:qtRSK insertion}\\[18.0pt]
{\rm RSK}&nonnegative integer matrices&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\lambda}{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}\times{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}&\text{open\end{array}}}}}}}}

Similarly to the classical dual RSK, the
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence also yields a tableaux theoretic proof of the skew dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials. The dual Pieri rule for Macdonald polynomials

(8)

P_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t)e_{r}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\succ^{\prime}\mu,\\
|\lambda/\mu|=r\end{subarray}}\varphi^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t).

can be obtained as the coefficient of
y_{1}^{r}
of the skew dual Cauchy identity (
36
) in the special case

P_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\prod_{i}(1+x_{i}y_{1})=\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(y_{1};t,q)\\
=\sum_{\lambda\succ^{\prime}\mu}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}y_{1}^{|\lambda/\mu|},

where the last equality is obtained by using the definition of
P_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(y_{1};t,q)
and the fact that
P_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(y_{1};t,q)=0
unless
\lambda/\mu
is a vertical strip. Hence it is immediate that
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
yields a tableaux theoretic proof of the dual Pieri rule for Macdonald polynomials. In particular, for each semistandard Young tableau
T
of shape
\mu
and sequence
(a_{i})
with
r
entries equal to
1
and all other entries
0
we obtain by
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
a probability distribution on semistandard Young tableaux
\widehat{T}
of shape
\lambda\succ^{\prime}\mu
by filling the following growth diagram according to our probabilistic local growth rules.

\emptyset
T^{(1)}
T^{(2)}
T^{(m-1)}
T^{(m)}
\emptyset
\widehat{T}^{(1)}
\widehat{T}^{(2)}
\widehat{T}^{(m-1)}
\widehat{T}^{(m)}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{m}

1.3.
Future work

One immediate question which we want to study in the future is whether there is an analogue extension of
q{\rm RS}t
to a
(q,t)
-variation of RSK. There already exists a
q
-deformation of row insertion and a
t
-variation of column insertion by
[
13
,
3
]
, which a hoped for
(q,t)
-variation of RSK would specialize to. Another direction is to provide a combinatorial proof for the fact that Macdonald functions, when defined by (
4
), are symmetric with respect to the
\mathbf{x}
-variables. In the case of Schur functions such a proof is obtained by either using the Bender–Knuth involution or the Lascoux–Schützenberger symmetric group action on semistandard Young tableaux. Again one would hope for a probabilistic variation generalizing one or both of these bijections.

For two weighted sets with equal sum of weights the forward and backward probabilities have to satisfy simple algebraic equations. For a generic situation this implies that there exist many probabilistic bijections between these two sets. The probabilities presented in this paper were obtained by regarding small examples, choosing the “reasonable” solution for these equations in each example, and then guessing a general formula. It was crucial that for all regarded examples there existed exactly one “reasonable” solution, where reasonable meant that the solution factorised into a minimal number of irreducible factors (this property is closely related to the notion of height functions in Diophantine geometry).
An open problem is to identify an additional property, e.g. the above described one, for probabilistic bijections and prove that the probabilistic bijections presented in this paper are unique with respect to this property.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

In §
2
, we introduce basic notions and show several approaches for proving the dual Cauchy identity (
2
). First we present the column version of dual RSK, continue with an algebraic proof using up and dual down operators on Young’s lattice, and finally show how Fomin’s growth diagrams can be used to “bijectivize” such an algebraic proof leading to a family of insertion algorithms generalizing dual RSK.

In §
3
, we review basic facts about Macdonald polynomials and define
(q,t)
-up and dual down operators.
We introduce in §
4.1
the notion of a probabilistic bijection and present in §
4.2
the forward and backward probabilities which yield a proof of the commutation relation of the
(q,t)
-up and dual down operators. In §
4.3
we give a second formulation of our probabilities which allows us to show that they actually generalize the classical dual RSK. In particular we obtain a lattice path description for the “maximal” monomial in
q
and
t
of the probabilities.
In §
4.4
we use probabilistic growth rules to define the
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence.

In §
5
, we prove that the forward and backward probabilities actually form a probabilistic bijection. The fact that these probabilities form probability distributions follows by a version of Lagrange interpolation presented in §
5.1
, while the compatibility condition is shown by introducing and analyzing a third formulation of the forward and backward probabilities in §
5.2
-
5.3
.

In §
6
, we consider various degenerations of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
by specializing
q
or
t
respectively; in particular we prove an interchanging property of the correspondence in the Jack limit. In §
7
we restrict
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
to
\{0,1\}
-matrices with at most one
1
entry per column and obtain a
(q,t)
-version of RS for words.

2.
Dual Cauchy identity, up and down operators, and growth diagrams

2.1.
Schur functions and the dual Cauchy identity

A
partition

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
is a weakly decreasing sequence of nonnegative integers. We say that
\lambda
is a partition of the integer
|\lambda|=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}
which is denoted by
\lambda\vdash|\lambda|
. We identify the partition
\lambda
with its
Young diagram
which is, following french convention, a collection of left-justified boxes consisting of
\lambda_{i}
boxes in the
i
-th row from bottom. Starting in §
4
we denote Young diagrams using
Quebecois convention
, in which the boxes are right-justified instead of left-justified. The boxes, or
cells
, of a Young diagram are indexed by positive Cartesian coordinates, i.e., the
x
-th cell in the
y
-th row from bottom is
c=(x,y)
. We denote by
\lambda^{\prime}
the
conjugate
of
\lambda
, which is obtained by reflecting the Young diagram of
\lambda
in the diagonal
x=y
.

We define for a cell
c=(x,y)\in\lambda
its
arm-length

a_{\lambda}(c)
and its
leg-length

\ell_{\lambda}(c)
by

a_{\lambda}(c)=\lambda_{y}-x,\qquad\qquad\ell_{\lambda}(c)=\lambda_{x}^{\prime}-y.

The
hook-length
of
c
is defined as
h_{\lambda}(c)=a_{\lambda}(c)+\ell_{\lambda}(c)+1
. The cell
c
as in Figure
1
has arm-length
a_{\lambda}(c)=5
, leg-length
\ell_{\lambda}(c)=3
, and hook-length
h_{\lambda}(c)=9
.

c
a_{\lambda}(c)
\ell_{\lambda}(c)

Figure 1
.
The Young diagram of the partition
\lambda=(7,6,3,2,1,1)
for which the cell
c=(2,1)
is marked.

Let
\lambda
be a partition. A filling of the cells of
\lambda
with positive integers is called a

•

semistandard Young tableau
(SSYT) of shape
\lambda
, if the rows are weakly increasing and columns are strictly increasing,

•

dual semistandard Young tableau
of shape
\lambda
, if the rows are strictly increasing and columns are weakly increasing,

•

partial standard Young tableau
of shape
\lambda
, if the rows and columns are strictly increasing and each entry appears at most once.

It is immediate, that semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
are in bijection to dual semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda^{\prime}
. We denote by
{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)
the set of all semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
, by
{\rm SSYT}^{*}(\lambda)
the set of all dual semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
and by
{\rm SYT}^{\leq n}(\lambda)
the set of all partial standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
and entries at most
n
. The
content
of an SSYT
T
is the sequence
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots)
, where
\mu_{i}
is the number of entries in
T
equal to
i
. The content of a dual semistandard Young tableau is defined analogously.

Example 2.1
.

The following is a semistandard Young tableau of shape
(6,5,3)
and content
(2,2,2,3,2,3)
.
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
be an infinite sequence of indeterminates. For a (dual) semistandard Young diagram
T
with content
\mu
, we set
\mathbf{x}^{T}=\prod_{i\geq 1}x_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
. The
Schur function

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
associated to the partition
\lambda
is defined by

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)}\mathbf{x}^{T}.

The next two theorems are fundamental results in the theory of symmetric functions.

Theorem 2.2

(Cauchy identity)
.

For two sequences of indeterminates
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
, we have

\prod_{i,j\geq 1}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}=\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y}),

where the sum is over all partitions
\lambda
.

The Cauchy identity can be rewritten in the form

(9)

\sum_{A=(a_{ij})}\prod_{i,j\geq 1}(x_{i}y_{j})^{a_{ij}}=\sum_{(P,Q)}\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q},

where the sum on the left-hand side runs over all nonnegative integer matrices with finite support (i.e., only finitely many nonzero entries), and the sum on the right-hand side runs over all ordered pairs of SSYTs of the same shape.

Theorem 2.3

(Dual Cauchy identity)
.

For two sequences of indeterminates
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
, we have

\prod_{i,j\geq 1}(1+x_{i}y_{j})=\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}),

where the sum is over all partitions
\lambda
.

Analogously to above, the dual Cauchy identity can be rewritten as

(10)

\sum_{A=(a_{ij})}\prod_{i,j\geq 1}(x_{i}y_{j})^{a_{ij}}=\sum_{(P,Q)}\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q},

where the sum on the left is over all finitely supported
\{0,1\}
-matrices, and the sum on the right is over all SSYTs
P
and dual SSYTs
Q
of the same shape.

The Cauchy and dual Cauchy identities can be proven combinatorially by the
Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK) correspondence
and the
dual RSK correspondence (RSK
∗
)
, respectively. Each of these bijections has a
row insertion version
and a
column insertion version
. In the remainder of this section, we recall the column insertion version of dual RSK.

The basic building block of the correspondence is the
insertion
of a number
k
into a column of a semistandard Young tableau: if
k
is larger than all entries in the regarded column, add
k
to the top of the column. Otherwise, let
z
be the bottom-most entry in the column which is larger than or equal to
k
, and replace
z
with
k
. We say that
z
is
bumped
out of the column.
Next, define the
column insertion
of a number
k
into an SSYT
P
, denoted
k\rightarrow P
, by the following process.

•

Insert
k
into the first (left-most) column of
P
. If no entry is bumped, the process terminates.

•

If
z
is bumped out of column
i
, insert
z
into column
i+1
. Continue in this manner until no bumping occurs.

Note that the shape of
k\rightarrow P
differs from the shape of
P
by the addition of a single cell.

Let
A
be a finitely supported
\{0,1\}
-matrix, and denote by
\omega_{A}
the
biword
obtained as follows. We read the columns of
A
from top to bottom, starting with the first column, and append
\begin{pmatrix}j\\
i\end{pmatrix}
to
\omega_{A}
if the entry in row
i
and column
j
is
1
. Given the biword

\omega_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&\cdots&j_{N}\\
i_{1}&i_{2}&\cdots&i_{N}\end{pmatrix},

we define
P
as the SSYT obtained by the sequence of column insertions
i_{N}\rightarrow i_{N-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow i_{1}\rightarrow\emptyset
. We define
Q
as the dual SSYT which records the growth of
P
by placing the number
j_{k}
in the cell that was added during the insertion of
i_{k}
. It is straightforward to see that this is indeed a dual SSYT. The tableaux
P
and
Q
are called the
insertion tableau
and
recording tableau
of
A
, respectively. One can show that these two tableaux provide enough information to reverse the insertion procedure and recover
A
. The map
A\mapsto(P,Q)
is therefore a bijection between finitely supported
\{0,1\}
-matrices and pairs of tableaux of the same shape, where the first tableau is semistandard and the second is dual semistandard. We refer to this bijection as the
column insertion version of the dual RSK
.

Example 2.4
.

Let
A
be the matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0&1\\
1&1&0&1&0\\
0&1&1&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The corresponding biword is

\omega_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&2&2&3&4&5\\
1&2&2&3&3&2&1\end{pmatrix}.

The process of constructing
P
and
Q
by the successive insertions

1\rightarrow 2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 2\rightarrow 2\rightarrow 1\rightarrow\emptyset

is shown next.

Note that the
row insertion version of dual RSK
is defined analogously with the difference that we use row insertion and that we construct the biword by reading the columns from bottom to top, starting with the first column.

2.2.
Up and down operators

In this section we explain an algebraic approach for proving the (dual) Cauchy identity which is based on linear operators on Young’s lattice. For more insights on these operators compare for example to
[
9
]
and
[
21
, §2.2-3.1]
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions. We write
\mu\subseteq\lambda
if the Young diagram of
\mu
is contained in the Young diagram of
\lambda
. Denote by
\lambda\cup\mu
(resp.,
\lambda\cap\mu
) the partition obtained by the union (resp., intersection) of the Young diagrams of
\lambda
and
\mu
.
Young’s lattice
is the partial order on partitions defined by the inclusion relation
\subseteq
; its meet and join are given by
\cap
and
\cup
, respectively.
For
\mu\subseteq\lambda
we denote by
\lambda/\mu
the
skew diagram
which consists of the cells in
\lambda
which are not included in
\mu
. We say that
\lambda/\mu
is a
horizontal strip
(resp., vertical strip) if no two cells of
\lambda/\mu
are in the same column (resp., row), where we use the notation
\mu\prec\lambda
(resp.,
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
).
Denote by
\mathbb{Y}
the set of all partitions and by
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Y}
the vector space generated by partitions, i.e.,

\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Y}:=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{Q}\lambda.

We define
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\rrbracket
as the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of formal power series in the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Y}
, i.e., the coefficient of each monomial
x^{i}y^{j}
is a finite formal sum over partitions. We define the
up operator

U_{x}
, the
down operator

D_{y}
and
dual down operator

D_{y}^{*}
as
\mathbb{Q}
-linear maps on
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
via

U_{x}\lambda=\sum_{\nu\succ\lambda}x^{|\nu/\lambda|}\nu,\qquad\qquad D_{y}\lambda=\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}y^{|\lambda/\mu|}\mu,\qquad\qquad D_{y}^{*}\lambda=\sum_{\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda}y^{|\lambda/\mu|}\mu.

Theorem 2.5
.

The up and (dual) down operator satisfy the commutation relations

(11)

\displaystyle D_{y}U_{x}=\frac{1}{1-xy}U_{x}D_{y},

(12)

\displaystyle D_{y}^{*}U_{x}=(1+xy)U_{x}D_{y}^{*}.

Before explaining the proof of the second commutation relation, we show how it implies the dual Cauchy identity for Schur polynomials; the Cauchy identity follows analogously. Denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the inner product on
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Y}
defined by
\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}
, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}
which we extend linearly to a
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rrbracket
-bilinear function from
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Y}
to
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rrbracket
. On the one hand, we have

\left\langle D^{*}_{y_{n}}\cdots D^{*}_{y_{1}}U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle=\sum_{\lambda}\langle U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\lambda\rangle\;\langle D^{*}_{y_{n}}\cdots D^{*}_{y_{1}}\lambda,\emptyset\rangle.

A semistandard Young tableau
P
of shape
\lambda
can be seen as a chain

\emptyset=P^{(0)}\prec P^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec P^{(m)}=\lambda,

in Young’s lattice, where
P^{(i)}
denotes the shape of the subtableau of
P
consisting of entries less than or equal to
i
. Further we have
\mathbf{x}^{P}=\linebreak\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{|P^{(i)}/P^{(i-1)}|}
. This implies that
\langle U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\lambda\rangle=s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
. Analogously, a dual semistandard Young tableau
Q
can be seen as a chain

\emptyset=Q^{(0)}\prec^{\prime}Q^{(1)}\prec^{\prime}\cdots\prec^{\prime}Q^{(n)}=\lambda.

On the other hand, we can repeatedly use the commutation relation to move a
D_{y_{j}}^{*}
past all
U_{x_{i}}
’s and obtain

D^{*}_{y_{j}}U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}=(1+x_{m}y_{j})U_{x_{m}}D^{*}_{y_{j}}U_{x_{m-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{m}(1+x_{i}y_{j})\cdot U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}D^{*}_{y_{j}}.

Since
D^{*}_{y}\emptyset=\emptyset
, we obtain by induction

\left\langle D^{*}_{y_{n}}\cdots D^{*}_{y_{1}}U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle=\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}(1+x_{i}y_{j})\left\langle U_{x_{m}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle\\
=\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}(1+x_{i}y_{j}),

which proves Theorem
2.3
when restricting to the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
.

Note that both commutation relations in Theorem
2.5
are special cases of the skew version of the (dual) Cauchy identity for Schur polynomials, compare to
[
12
, Ch. 1, Eq. (5.8 a)]
.In the remainder of the section we present a combinatorial proof of the commutation relation (
12
) for the up and dual down operator.

Define the sets
\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho):=\{\nu|\nu\succ^{\prime}\lambda,\nu\succ\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=k\}
and
\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho):=\{\mu|\mu\prec\lambda,\mu\prec^{\prime}\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|=k\}
. In order to prove (
12
) it suffices to show

(13)

|\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)|=|\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)|+|\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)|.

for all partitions
\lambda,\rho
and nonnegative integers
k
.

(a)

The partition
\lambda\cap\rho
together with all inner corners (colored in orange).

(b)

The partition
\lambda\cap\rho
where only the
removable
inner corners are shown.

(c)

The partition
\lambda\cup\rho
together with all outer corners (colored in blue).

(d)

The partition
\lambda\cup\rho
where only
addable
outer corners are shown.

Figure 2
.
The (removable) inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
and the (addable) outer corners of
\lambda\cup\rho
for the partitions
\lambda=(9,9,7,5,5,4,2)
and
\rho=(10,7,7,6,6,2,2,1)
. We shaded the cells of
\lambda/(\lambda\cap\rho)
horizontally in magenta and the cells of
\rho/(\lambda\cap\rho)
vertically in green.

An
inner corner
of a partition
\lambda
is a cell
c\in\lambda
such that
\lambda/\mu=\{c\}
for a partition
\mu\prec\lambda
. An
outer corner
of
\lambda
is a cell
c\notin\lambda
such that
\nu/\lambda=\{c\}
for a partition
\nu
with
\lambda\prec\nu
. We call an inner corner
c
of
\lambda\cap\rho

removable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if
\lambda/\mu
is a horizontal strip and
\rho/\mu
is a vertical strip, where
(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu=\{c\}
.
Analogously we call an outer corner
c
of
\lambda\cup\rho

addable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if
\nu/\lambda
is a vertical strip and
\nu/\rho
is a horizontal strip, where
\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)=\{c\}
.
For an example see Figure
2
. For both removable and addable corners we omit referring to
(\lambda,\rho)
whenever the partitions
\lambda,\rho
are clear from context.

Each partition
\nu
in
\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)
corresponds to a
k
-subset of the addable outer corners of
\lambda\cup\rho
and each partition
\mu
in
\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)
corresponds to a
k
-subset of the removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
. It is not difficult to prove that there is one more addable outer corner of
(\lambda\cup\rho)
than removable inner corner of
(\lambda\cap\rho)
. This implies immediately the assertion on the sizes of the sets in (
13
). As we will see in the next subsection, it turns out to be fruitful to prove (
13
) explicitly bijective.
For each
\lambda,\rho
and
k
, we choose a bijection

F_{\lambda,\rho,k}:\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho).

Two of the many possible bijections
F_{\lambda,\rho,k}
are very natural: the
dual row insertion bijection

F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm row}
and the
dual column insertion bijection

F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col}
. For
k=1
the dual row (resp., column) insertion bijection maps a removable inner corner to the next addable outer corner in a row above (resp., column to the right) and sends the empty set to the lowest (resp., left-most) addable outer corner. Figure
3
illustrates this case. For
k>1
both maps are defined recursively by

(14)

F^{\bullet}_{\lambda,\rho,k}(X)=\begin{cases}\bigcup\limits_{x\in X}F^{\bullet}_{\lambda,\rho,1}(\{x\})\qquad&|X|=k,\\
F^{\bullet}_{\lambda,\rho,1}(\emptyset)\cup\bigcup\limits_{x\in X}F^{\bullet}_{\lambda,\rho,1}(\{x\})&|X|=k-1,\end{cases}

where
F^{\bullet}_{\lambda,\rho,k}
stands for
F^{\rm row}_{\lambda,\rho,k}
or
F^{\rm col}_{\lambda,\rho,k}
respectively.

F^{\rm row}_{\lambda,\rho,1}
F^{\rm col}_{\lambda,\rho,1}

Figure 3
.
The two maps
F^{\rm row}_{\lambda,\rho,1}
(left) and
F^{\rm col}_{\lambda,\rho,1}
(right) for
\lambda=(9,9,7,5,5,4,2)
,
\rho=(10,7,7,6,6,2,2,1)
. The removable inner corners (colored in orange) and the addable outer corners (colored in blue) are obtained in Figure
2
.

2.3.
Growth diagrams

In this section we review Fomin’s growth diagrams
[
8
,
7
]
which provide a mechanism for turning a bijective proof of the commutation relations in Theorem
2.5
into a bijective proof of the (dual) Cauchy identity. We explain how each bijective proof arising in this way can be interpreted as an insertion algorithm, with the row and column versions of the dual Robinson–Schensted correspondence as special cases. We only consider growth diagrams for
\{0,1\}
-matrices; for the generalization to nonnegative integer matrices and the RSK and Burge correspondences, we refer the reader to
[
21
, §2.2, §3.1-3.2]
.

Let
A
be an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix. We view
A
as an
m\times n
grid of squares, and consider labelings of the vertices of this grid with partitions. We index the squares of the grid by
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
, and the vertices by
(i,j)\in[0,m]\times[0,n]
; these indices are interpreted as matrix coordinates, rather than Cartesian coordinates.

Definition 2.6
.

Fix an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix
A
. A
dual growth associated with
A
is an assignment
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{ij})
of partitions to the vertices
(i,j)\in[0,m]\times[0,n]
, such that

•

if
i<m
,
\Lambda_{i+1,j}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

if
j<n
,
\Lambda_{i,j+1}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a vertical strip,

•

|\Lambda_{ij}|
is equal to the number of
1
’s in
A
to the northwest of the vertex
(i,j)
, that is,

|\Lambda_{i,j}|=\sum_{1\leq k\leq i\atop 1\leq\ell\leq j}A_{k,\ell}.

It is clear that the matrix
A
is determined by the partitions
\Lambda_{i,j}
, so we will often omit reference to
A
and simply refer to
\Lambda
as a
dual growth
. This definition can be traced back to the notion of “two-dimensional growth” introduced by Fomin
[
8
]
.

Example 2.7
.

There are three dual growths associated with the matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&1\end{pmatrix}
:

\emptyset

Above we represent the partitions
\Lambda_{i,j}
by their Young diagrams. Also, for purposes of readability, we represent the 1’s in
A
with
X
’s and omit the 0’s.

Given a dual growth
\Lambda
, let
P(\Lambda)
be the semistandard tableau of shape
\lambda=\Lambda_{m,n}
determined by the last column

\emptyset=\Lambda_{0,n}\prec\Lambda_{1,n}\prec\cdots\prec\Lambda_{m,n}=\lambda,

and let
Q(\Lambda)
be the dual semistandard tableau of shape
\lambda
determined by the last row

\emptyset=\Lambda_{m,0}\prec^{\prime}\Lambda_{m,1}\prec^{\prime}\cdots\prec^{\prime}\Lambda_{m,n}=\lambda.

For example, the second dual growth in Example
2.7
has

P(\Lambda)=\hbox to45.12pt{\vbox to42.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.21985pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.11316pt}{3.89096pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

If
\Lambda
is associated with
A
and
P=P(\Lambda),Q=Q(\Lambda)
, we write

\Lambda:A\rightarrow(P,Q),

and say that “
\Lambda
is a dual growth from
A
to
(P,Q)
.” In general, there may be multiple dual growths from
A
to
(P,Q)
.

By definition, each square in a dual growth has the form

(15)

\hbox to111.34pt{\vbox to69.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.44542pt\lower-48.91801pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\text{with}\quad a\in\{0,1\},\quad\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k-a}(\lambda,\rho),

where
k=|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+a
.

Definition 2.8
.

A set of
local dual growth rules

F_{\bullet}
is a choice of bijections

F_{\lambda,\rho,k}:\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho),

for all ordered pairs of partitions
(\lambda,\rho)
, and all
k\geq 0
. A dual growth
\Lambda
is an

F_{\bullet}
-dual growth diagram
if each square satisfies
\nu=F_{\lambda,\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+a}(\mu)
, where
a,\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu
are as in (
15
).

The key property of local dual growth rules is that they make the process of constructing dual growths deterministic. More precisely, if
\Lambda
is an
F_{\bullet}
-dual growth diagram, then for each square, the partition
\nu
is determined by the partitions
\mu,\lambda,\rho
and the value of
a
. Conversely, the pair
(a,\mu)
is determined by the partitions
\lambda,\rho,\nu

(16)

\mu=F_{\lambda,\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|}^{-1}(\nu),\qquad\quad a=|\nu|+|\mu|-|\lambda|-|\rho|.

This implies that the set of
F_{\bullet}
-dual growth diagrams is in bijection with both
\{0,1\}
-matrices and pairs
(P,Q)
of tableaux of the same shape, where
P
is semistandard and
Q
is dual semistandard. Given a
\{0,1\}
-matrix
A
, one constructs the unique
F_{\bullet}
-dual growth diagram associated with
A
by filling the top row and left column of the grid with the empty partition, and using the bijections
F_{\lambda,\rho,k}
, along with the positions of 1’s in the matrix
A
, to recursively fill in the rest of the grid. Similarly, given a semistandard tableau
P
and a dual semistandard tableau
Q
of the same shape, one fills the right and bottom edges of the grid with the chains in Young’s lattice corresponding to
P
and
Q
, respectively, and then recursively fills in the rest of the grid (and the entries of the matrix) using (
16
). Thus, each set of local dual growth rules induces a different bijective proof of the dual Cauchy identity.

Remark 2.9
.

In effect, the framework of dual growth diagrams transforms a bijective proof of the commutation relation (
12
) into a bijective proof of the dual Cauchy identity by “bijectivizing” the algebraic argument given in §
2.2
.

We denote by
{\rm RSK}^{*}_{F_{\bullet}}
the bijection
A\mapsto(P,Q)
induced by the local dual growth rules
F_{\bullet}
. We now explain how to translate
{\rm RSK}^{*}_{F_{\bullet}}
into an insertion algorithm. Let
A
be a
\{0,1\}
-matrix, and
\Lambda
the
F_{\bullet}
-dual growth diagram associated with
A
. Suppose that the
j
th column of
\Lambda
looks like

T^{(0)}
T^{(1)}
T^{(m-1)}
T^{(m)}
\widehat{T}^{(0)}
\widehat{T}^{(1)}
\widehat{T}^{(m-1)}
\widehat{T}^{(m)}
A_{1,j}
A_{m,j}

for semistandard tableaux
T
and
\widehat{T}
. Let
\{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{r}\}
be the set of rows
i
for which
A_{i,j}=1
.

Lemma 2.10
.

In the situation described above, the tableau
\widehat{T}
is obtained as follows:

•

Initialize the
insertion queue
as the multiset
3
3

3

Note that at the beginning the insertion queue is actually a set, however it may contain elements with multiplicity at a later point of the algorithm.

\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
.

•

Let
i
be the smallest integer of the insertion queue. Denote by
C
the set of cells of
F_{\lambda,\rho,k}(\mu)/(\lambda\cup\rho)
where
\lambda=T^{(i)}
,
\rho=\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}
,
\mu=T^{(i-1)}
and
k=|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+A_{i,j}
. Place all
i
’s of the insertion queue into the cells of
C
, delete all
i
’s from the insertion queue and add all entries which have been replaced (bumped) in the current step to the insertion queue.

•

Repeat the previous step until the insertion queue is empty.

Proof.

For the proof we regard the following variation of the above algorithm: in the
i
-th insertion step we apply the above procedure if
i
is in the insertion queue and do nothing otherwise.

By definition
T^{(i)}
is the subtableau of
T
with entries at most
i
and
T^{(i)}/T^{(i-1)}
is the set of cells with entry
i
in
T
. Hence
(T^{(i)}\cap\widehat{T}^{(i-1)})/T^{(i-1)}
is the set of cells where an entry
i
was bumped during the first
(i-1)
insertion steps. Since each bumped entry is added to the insertion queue and the insertion queue contains at the beginning all
i
with
A_{i,j}=1
, the multiplicity of an entry
i
in the insertion queue after the
(i-1)
-st insertion step is equal to
k=|(T^{(i)}\cap\widehat{T}^{(i-1)})/T^{(i-1)}|+A_{i,j}
. By definition of the local
F_{\bullet}
-dual growth rules, we have
\widehat{T}^{(i)}=T^{(i)}\cup\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}
if
k=0
, i.e., we do nothing in this step. If
i
is in the insertion queue, we have
k>0
and by definition of the local
F_{\bullet}
- dual growth rules
\widehat{T}^{(i)}=F_{T^{(i)},\widehat{T}^{(i-1)},k}(T^{(i-1)})
. This implies
k=|T^{(i)}/(T^{(i)}\cup\widehat{T}^{(i-1)})|
, i.e, we fill each cell of
C
by one of the
i
entries of the insertion queue. This proves the claim.
∎

If
T
is a semistandard Young tableau and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}
are integers, we define the

F_{\bullet}
-insertion
of
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}
into
T
by the algorithm of Lemma
2.10
. For a given
\{0,1\}
-matrix
A
denote by
i_{1}^{(j)}<\cdots<i_{r_{j}}^{(j)}
the rows for which
A
has a
1
entry in the
j
-th column. It follows from the preceding discussion that if
{\rm RSK}^{*}_{F_{\bullet}}(A)=(P,Q)
, then
P
can be obtained by the successive
F_{\bullet}
-insertion of
i_{1}^{(j)},\ldots,i_{r_{j}}^{(j)}
, starting with
j=1
, into the empty tableau, and
Q
records the growth of
P
, just as for usual dual RSK insertion.

We call the
F_{\bullet}
-insertion
traceable
if we can keep track of where a bumped entry in the above algorithm is inserted again and instead of “simultaneously” inserting
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}
we can apply the insertion process for
i_{1}
, then for
i_{2}
etc. sequentially. This means that in order to obtain the insertion tableau, we can replace a column with
r
entries
1
in the rows
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}
by
r
columns where we have a
1
in the
i_{j}
-th row of the
j
-th new column. Analogously we define the
F_{\bullet}
-insertion
reverse traceable
if the sequential insertion is in the reverse ordering, i.e. first insert
i_{r}
, then
i_{r-1}
until finally
i_{1}
.
It is not difficult to convince oneself by using (
14
), that the dual column insertion
F_{\bullet}^{\rm col}
is traceable while the dual row insertion
F_{\bullet}^{\rm row}
is reverse traceable. Hence it is immediate that they correspond to the usual row or column insertion version of
{\rm RSK}^{*}
.

2.4
.

Given a set of local dual growth rules
F_{\bullet}
we define the
transpose

F_{\bullet}^{\prime}
of the local dual growth rules by

(17)

F^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},k}(\mu^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}\qquad:\Leftrightarrow\qquad F_{\lambda,\rho,k}(\mu)=\nu.

It is immediate that the transpose
\left(F^{{\rm col}}_{\bullet}\right)^{\prime}
of the column insertion is the row insertion
F^{{\rm row}}_{\bullet}
and vice-versa.
By definition we obtain the following symmetry for the
{F_{\bullet}}
-
{\rm RSK}^{*}

(18)

{\rm RSK}^{*}_{F_{\bullet}}(A)=(P,Q)\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad{\rm RSK}^{*}_{F_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(A^{T})=(Q^{\prime},P^{\prime}).

3.
Background on Macdonald polynomials

In this section we review certain basic properties of Macdonald polynomials, following
[
12
, Ch. VI]
.

The
Macdonald symmetric functions

P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
are symmetric functions in an infinite set of variables
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
with coefficients in the field
\mathbb{Q}(q,t)
of rational functions in two additional variables
q
and
t
. They were originally defined as the orthogonal basis obtained by applying the Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization procedure to the basis of monomial symmetric functions (ordered by dominance order) with respect to a certain inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{q,t}
that depends on
q
and
t
. We denote by
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
the elements of the basis dual to the
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
. We usually refer to the symmetric functions
P_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\lambda}
as
Macdonald polynomials
, even though they are not polynomials over any ring. Macdonald polynomials generalize many families of symmetric functions. Of importance to this paper are the

q
-Whittaker functions

W_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q)
, the
Hall–Littlewood functions

P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};t)
, the
Jack polynomials

P_{\lambda}(x;\alpha)
, and the Schur functions
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
, which are obtained from the Macdonald polynomials by

\displaystyle W_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q)

\displaystyle=P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,0),

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};t)

\displaystyle=P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};0,t),

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(x;\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};t^{\alpha},t),

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,q).

It follows easily from the definition of the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{q,t}
that the Macdonald polynomials satisfy a generalization of the Cauchy identity for Schur functions.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
12
, Ch. VI (4.13)]
)
.

Let
\textbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\textbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
be two sets of variables. Then

(19)

\prod_{i,j\geq 1}\dfrac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}=\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\textbf{x};q,t)Q_{\lambda}(\textbf{y};q,t),

where
(\alpha;q)_{\infty}=(1-\alpha)(1-\alpha q)(1-\alpha q^{2})\cdots
is the
infinite
q
-Pochhammer symbol
.

Using the automorphism
\omega_{q,t}
which can be defined as

\omega_{q,t}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x};t,q),

and its properties one obtains immediately the dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
12
, Ch. VI (5.4)]
)
.

Let
\textbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\textbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots)
be two sets of variables. Then

(20)

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q)

(21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q).

With a good deal of effort, Macdonald was able to derive explicit formulas for the monomial expansions of
P_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\lambda}
as weighted sums over semistandard Young tableaux. In order to describe these expansions we need some notations. For a partition
\lambda
and a cell
c
, define

h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)=1-q^{a_{\lambda}(c)}t^{\ell_{\lambda}(c)+1},\qquad h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)=1-q^{a_{\lambda}(c)+1}t^{\ell_{\lambda}(c)},

if
c\in\lambda
, and
h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)=h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)=1
if
c\not\in\lambda
. We refer to both of these polynomials as

(q,t)
-hook-lengths
. Further we need their ratio which is denoted by
b_{\lambda}(c)

b_{\lambda}(c)=\dfrac{h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)}{h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)}.

For
\mu\subseteq\lambda
, define

\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}}\dfrac{b_{\mu}(c)}{b_{\lambda}(c)},\qquad\qquad\varphi^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}}\dfrac{b_{\lambda}(c)}{b_{\mu}(c)},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}
) is the set of all cells in
\lambda
which are in the same row (resp., column) as a cell of
\lambda/\mu
. This is the definition used by Macdonald
4
4

4

Contrary to Macdonald
[
12
, Ch. VI (6.24)]
we use the symbol
\varphi^{*}
instead of
\psi^{\prime}
.
and differs from the one in
[
1
]
.

*
*
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}
c_{4}
c_{3}
c_{2}
c_{1}
c_{6}
c_{5}
c_{7}

Figure 4
.
For
\lambda=(6,5,3,2)
and
\mu=(6,4,3,1)
the skew diagram
\lambda/\mu
consists of the cells with a star entry. The cells of
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}
are colored blue and red, respectively.

Example 3.3
.

For
\lambda=(6,5,3,2)
and
\mu=(6,4,3,1)
as in Figure
4
, we have

\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)=\dfrac{b_{\mu}(c_{1})b_{\mu}(c_{2})b_{\mu}(c_{3})b_{\mu}(c_{4})}{b_{\lambda}(c_{1})b_{\lambda}(c_{2})b_{\lambda}(c_{3})b_{\lambda}(c_{4})}\\
=\dfrac{(1-t)^{2}(1-q^{2})^{2}(1-qt^{2})(1-q^{3}t)(1-q^{3}t^{3})(1-q^{5}t^{2})}{(1-q)^{2}(1-qt)^{2}(1-q^{2}t)(1-q^{2}t^{2})(1-q^{4}t^{2})(1-q^{4}t^{3})},

\varphi^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)=\dfrac{b_{\lambda}(c_{5})b_{\lambda}(c_{6})b_{\lambda}(c_{7})}{b_{\mu}(c_{5})b_{\mu}(c_{6})b_{\mu}(c_{7})}=\dfrac{(1-q^{2})^{2}(1-qt^{2})^{2}(1-q^{5}t^{2})(1-q^{4}t^{4})}{(1-qt)^{2}(1-q^{2}t)^{2}(1-q^{4}t^{3})(1-q^{5}t^{3})}.

For a semistandard Young tableau
T
and a dual semistandard Young tableau
T^{*}
, define the rational functions
\psi_{T}(q,t),\varphi^{*}_{T^{*}}(q,t)
by

\psi_{T}(q,t)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\psi_{T^{(i)}/T^{(i-1)}}(q,t),\qquad\qquad\varphi^{*}_{T^{*}}(q,t)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\varphi_{T^{*(i)}/T^{*(i-1)}}(q,t),

where, as in previous sections,
T^{(i)}
and
T^{*(i)}
denotes the shape of the subtableau of
T
or
T^{*}
respectively consisting of entries less than or equal to
i
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
12
, Ch. VI (
7.13
)]
)
.

The Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
has the following monomial expansions over semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda

(22)

P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)}\psi_{T}(q,t)\textbf{x}^{T}.

The above monomial expansion of
P_{\lambda}
over SSYTs immediately implies an expansion over dual SSYTs

(23)

P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x};t,q)=\sum_{T^{*}\in{\rm SSYT}^{*}(\lambda)}\varphi^{*}_{T^{*}}(q,t)\textbf{x}^{T^{*}},

by using that
\varphi^{*}_{\kappa/\rho}(q,t)=\psi_{\kappa^{\prime}/\rho^{\prime}}(t,q)
holds for any
\rho\subset\kappa
.
The Macdonald polynomials
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
can be expressed analogously as shown in (
58
).

In this paper, we take the somewhat unusual perspective of viewing Theorem
3.4
as the
definition
of the Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}
. The theory of Schur functions can be developed in elegant combinatorial fashion by taking the monomial expansion over semistandard Young tableaux as the starting point (for example, this is Stanley’s approach in
[
20
, Ch. 7]
). We believe that trying to mimic this approach in the more general Macdonald setting will lead to interesting combinatorial and probabilistic results.

3.1.
Up and down operators for Macdonald polynomials

In §
2.2
and §
2.3
we showed that the dual Cauchy identity for Schur polynomials can be proven combinatorially by “bijectivizing” the algebraic proof via up and dual down operators. We now the take the first step towards an analogous proof in the Macdonald setting by providing the generalized algebraic setting.

We define the

(q,t)
-up operator
and

(q,t)
-dual down operator
as

U_{z}(q,t)\lambda=\sum_{\nu\succ\lambda}z^{|\nu/\lambda|}\psi_{\nu/\lambda}(q,t)\;\nu,\quad\quad D_{z}^{*}(q,t)\lambda=\sum_{\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda}z^{|\lambda/\mu|}\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}^{*}(q,t)\;\mu.

Theorem 3.5
.

The
(q,t)
-up and
(q,t)
-dual down operators satisfy the commutation relation

(24)

D^{*}_{y}(q,t)U_{x}(q,t)=(1+xy)U_{x}(q,t)D^{*}_{y}(q,t).

This is in fact equivalent to the skew dual Cauchy identity in the special case of
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1})
, compare to
[
12
, Ch. VI, Ex 6(c)]
or (
36
), but we will take the opposite perspective: we seek to prove this identity with a probabilistic bijection, and then use it to deduce the dual Cauchy identity (which implies the skew dual Cauchy identity by a standard argument).

The commutation relation of the up and dual down operator is equivalent to the skew version of the dual Cauchy identity in the special case of
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1})
, compare to
[
12
, Ch. VI, Ex 6(c)]
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}),\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
be two finite sets of indeterminates. Then

\prod_{1\leq i\leq m\atop 1\leq j\leq n}(1+x_{i}y_{j})=\sum_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q),

where the sum is over all partitions
\lambda
.

Proof.

It is immediate by the definition of the
(q,t)
-up and
(q,t)
-dual down operator and the monomial expansions of
P_{\lambda}
in (
22
) and (
23
) that

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)

\displaystyle=\langle U_{x_{m}}(q,t)\cdots U_{x_{1}}(q,t)\emptyset,\lambda\rangle,

\displaystyle P_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q)

\displaystyle=\langle D^{*}_{y_{1}}(q,t)\cdots D^{*}_{y_{n}}(q,t)\lambda,\emptyset\rangle.

Now we just repeatedly apply (
24
) in the usual way.
∎

In §
2.2
we defined the two sets
\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho):=\{\nu|\nu\succ^{\prime}\lambda,\nu\succ\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=k\}
and
\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho):=\{\mu|\mu\prec\lambda,\mu\prec^{\prime}\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|=k\}
. In order to prove the commutation relation (
24
) we need to show

(25)

\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)}\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}(q,t)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)}\psi_{\nu/\rho}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\nu/\lambda}(q,t),

for any partitions
\lambda
and
\rho
and integer
k\geq 0
.
For
\lambda=\rho
the partitions
\nu
and
\mu
in the above sums have to be
diagonal strips
(i.e., no two boxes in the same row or column) with respect to
\lambda
, i.e.,
\nu/\lambda
and
\lambda/\mu
are both diagonal strips. When further
k=1
, this is equivalent to the single box case of the
(q,t)
-up-down commutation relation for which we have presented a probabilistic bijection in
[
1
]
. In general, each side of this identity has
\binom{d+1}{k}
summands, where
d
is the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
\lambda=(h^{v},1)
and
\rho=(h+1,h^{v-1})
be almost rectangular partitions with
h\geq 3
and
v\geq 2
. The partitions
\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{1}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{0}(\lambda,\rho)
and
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{1}(\lambda,\rho)
are shown below (for
h=8,v=4
) together with their weights
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}(q,t)
and
\psi_{\nu/\rho}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\nu/\lambda}(q,t)
.

\psi_{\lambda/\mu}\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}

\dfrac{(1-t^{v-1})(1-q^{h-1})}{(1-qt^{v-2})(1-q^{h-2}t)}

\dfrac{(1-q^{h-2}t^{v})(1-q^{h-1})}{(1-q^{h-2}t)(1-q^{h-1}t^{v-1})}

4.
Probabilistic growth rules

4.1.
Probabilistic bijections

The following definition is due to Bufetov and Petrov
[
5
]
, although they use the term
“bijectivization” (or “coupling”) rather than “probabilistic bijection.”

Let
X
and
Y
be finite sets equipped with weight functions
\omega:X\rightarrow A
,
\overline{\omega}:Y\rightarrow A
, where
A
is an algebra. A
probabilistic bijection
from
(X,\omega)
to
(Y,\overline{\omega})
is a pair of maps
\mathcal{P},\overline{\mathcal{P}}:X\times Y\rightarrow A
satisfying

(1)

For each
x\in X
,
\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\mathcal{P}(x,y)=1
.

(2)

For each
y\in Y
,
\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)=1
.

(3)

For each
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
\displaystyle\omega(x)\mathcal{P}(x,y)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)\overline{\omega}(y)
.

For the remainder of the paper we write
\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)
for
\mathcal{P}(x,y)
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)
for
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
. We think of
\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)
as the “probability” of mapping
x
to
y
and call it therefore the
forward probability
and of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)
as the “probability” of mapping
y
“back” to
x
and call it the
backward probability
. We put “probability” in quotes because we do not require
\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y),\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)\in[0,1]
(they need not even be real-valued).
Condition (1) states that
\mathcal{P}
defines a “probability distribution” on
Y
for each
x
, and (2) says that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
defines a “probability distribution” on
X
for each
y
. We refer to (3) as the
compatibility condition
.

The next lemma shows, that analogously to bijections, it satisfies to find a forward probability and a backward probability, i.e., a probabilistic bijection, in order to prove that two weighted sets have equal sums of weights.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
\mathcal{P},\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a probabilistic bijection between
(X,\omega)
and
(Y,\overline{\omega})
, then

\sum_{x\in X}\omega(x)=\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\omega}(y).

Proof.

Using properties (1), (3), and (2) successively, we compute

\sum_{x\in X}\omega(x)=\sum_{x\in X}\sum_{y\in Y}\omega(x)\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)=\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)\overline{\omega}(y)=\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\omega}(y).\qed

The existence of a probabilistic bijection
\mathcal{P},\overline{\mathcal{P}}
also implies the more refined identities

\sum_{x\in X}\omega(x)\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)=\overline{\omega}(y),\qquad\qquad\sum_{y\in Y}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)\overline{\omega}(y)=\omega(x).

If
\omega(x)=\overline{\omega}(y)=1
for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, and
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a bijection, then we may take
\mathcal{P}(x\rightarrow y)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(x\leftarrow y)=\delta_{y,f(x)}
. Thus, the notion of probabilistic bijection generalizes that of bijection, allowing for situations in which
X
and
Y
have different cardinalities, or the same cardinality but differently distributed weight functions, etc.
Further we want to point out, that there is an easy connection between the concept of probabilistic bijections and joint distributions, compare for example with
[
1
, Remark 4.1.4]
.

4.2.
The probabilities

For partitions
\lambda,\rho,\mu,\nu
satisfying
\mu\prec\lambda\prec^{\prime}\nu
and
\mu\prec^{\prime}\rho\prec\nu
we define the weights

\displaystyle\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)=\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\varphi_{\rho/\mu}^{*}(q,t),\qquad\qquad\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)=\psi_{\nu/\rho}(q,t)\varphi_{\nu/\lambda}^{*}(q,t).

Then equation (
25
) becomes

(26)

\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)}\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)}\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu).

In order to show this equation, it “suffices” by Lemma
4.1
to find a forward probability
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)
and a backward probability
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)
for which we can prove

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)}\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)

\displaystyle=1\qquad\text{ for all }\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho),

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)}\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)

\displaystyle=1\qquad\text{ for all }\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho),

and

\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)=\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu),

for all
\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)\cup\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(\lambda,\rho)
and
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)
. In case we want to emphasize the dependence of the probabilities on
q
and
t
, we write
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)[q,t]
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)[q,t]
.
Before we can define these probabilities, we need to introduce some notations.

Denote by
d
the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
; see §
2.2
for their definition. For a subset
\mathbf{R}\subseteq[d]=\{1,2,\ldots d\}
we define the partition
\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}
as the partition obtained by removing from
\lambda\cap\rho
the
i
-th removable inner corner of
\lambda\cap\rho
, counted from bottom to top, for all
i\in\mathbf{R}
. For a subset
\mathbf{S}\subseteq[0,d]=\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
we define
\nu^{(\mathbf{S})}
as the partition obtained by adding to
\lambda\cup\rho
the
i
-th addable (“supplementable”) outer corner of
\lambda\cup\rho
, where we count the addable outer corners again from bottom to top but starting with
0
.

As we see in a moment, it turns out to be convenient to draw Young diagrams using
Quebecois convention
in which the boxes are right-justified instead of left-justified, i.e., one obtains this new convention by reflecting diagrams in French convention vertically or reflecting diagrams in English convention both vertically and horizontally, see Figure
5
.
We define
R_{i}
(resp.,
\overline{R}_{i}
) to be the lower right (resp., upper left) corner of the
i
-th removable inner corner of
\lambda\cap\rho
,
S_{i}
(resp.,
\overline{S}_{i}
) to be the lower right (resp., upper left) corner of the
i
-th addable outer corner of
\lambda\cup\rho
, and set
I_{i}=\overline{R}_{i}
and
O_{i}=S_{i}
. For an example see Figure
5
.

For the rest of the paper we identify a point with coordinates
(x,y)
with the monomial
5
5

5

By using French instead of Quebecois notation, the corresponding monomial would be
q^{-x}t^{y}
, and
q^{-x}t^{-y}
for English notation.

q^{x}t^{y}
. In order to determine the coordinates of the above defined points we assume that the cells of Young diagrams are unit squares and define the origin such that all of the above points have integer coordinates. Since the expressions we are interested in are homogeneous rational functions of degree 0 in the above defined points, these expressions are invariant under translation of the points and hence well-defined.

For
\mathbf{R}\subseteq[d]
and
\mathbf{S}\subseteq[0,d]
, we define the probabilities

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}\rightarrow\nu^{(\mathbf{S})})

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(S_{s}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}(S_{s}-O_{j})}\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}(R_{r}-O_{j})}{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(R_{r}-I_{i})},

(28)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}\leftarrow\nu^{(\mathbf{S})})

\displaystyle=\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(\overline{S}_{s}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}(\overline{S}_{s}-O_{j})}\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}(\overline{R}_{r}-O_{j})}{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(\overline{R}_{r}-I_{i})}.

If
\lambda,\rho
are clear from the context, we abbreviate the probabilities by

p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}\rightarrow\nu^{(\mathbf{S})}),\qquad\qquad\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}\leftarrow\nu^{(\mathbf{S})}).

When we want to emphasize the dependence of our probabilities on
q
and
t
, we write
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
or
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}\rightarrow\nu^{(\mathbf{S})})[q,t]
.

+
+
+
-
-
S_{0}=O_{0}
\overline{S}_{0}
S_{1}=O_{1}
\overline{S}_{1}
S_{2}=O_{2}
\overline{S}_{2}
R_{1}
\overline{R}_{1}=I_{1}
R_{2}
\overline{R}_{2}=I_{2}

Figure 5
.
The partition
\lambda\cup\rho
together with the points
I_{i},O_{j},R_{i},\overline{R}_{i},S_{j}
and
\overline{S}_{j}
for
\lambda=(9,9,7,5,5,4,2)
and
\rho=(10,7,7,6,6,2,2,1)
as in Figure
2
.

Example 4.2
.

For
\lambda=\rho=(2,1)
the points
I_{i},R_{i}
and
S_{j}=O_{j}
are shown next
S_{0}
S_{1}
S_{2}
R_{1}
R_{2}
I_{1}
I_{2}

For
\mathbf{R}=\{2\}
we have the following forward probabilities.

\displaystyle p_{\{2\},\{0,1\}}=\mathcal{P}_{(2,1),(2,1)}\left((2)\rightarrow(3,2)\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(S_{0}-I_{1})(S_{1}-I_{1})}{(S_{0}-O_{2})(S_{1}-O_{2})}\cdot\dfrac{(R_{2}-O_{2})}{(R_{2}-I_{1})}

\displaystyle=q\dfrac{(1-t)^{2}(1-q)}{(1-q^{2}t^{2})(1-qt)(1-q^{2})},

\displaystyle p_{\{2\},\{1,2\}}=\mathcal{P}_{(2,1),(2,1)}\left((2)\rightarrow(2,2,1)\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(S_{1}-I_{1})(S_{2}-I_{1})}{(S_{1}-O_{0})(S_{2}-O_{0})}\cdot\dfrac{(R_{2}-O_{0})}{(R_{2}-I_{1})}

\displaystyle=t\dfrac{(1-q)(1-q^{2}t)^{2}}{(1-qt)(1-q^{2}t^{2})(1-q^{2})},

\displaystyle p_{\{2\},\{0,2\}}=\mathcal{P}_{(2,1),(2,1)}\left((2)\rightarrow(3,1,1)\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(S_{0}-I_{1})(S_{2}-I_{1})}{(S_{0}-O_{1})(S_{2}-O_{1})}\cdot\dfrac{(R_{2}-O_{1})}{(R_{2}-I_{1})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(1-t)(1-q^{2}t)(1-q)}{(1-qt)^{2}(1-q^{2})}.

For an integer
k\geq 0
and a set
\mathbf{S}
, we denote by
\binom{\mathbf{S}}{k}
the set of
k
-element subsets of
\mathbf{S}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be partitions,
d
the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
, and
k\in[d+1]
. The probabilities satisfy

(29)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}}p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=1\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ for each }\mathbf{R}\in\binom{[d]}{k-1}\cup\binom{[d]}{k},

(30)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\binom{[d]}{k-1}\cup\binom{[d]}{k}}\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=1\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ for each }\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be partitions,
d
the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
, and
k\in[d+1]
. For
\mathbf{R}\in\binom{[d]}{k-1}\cup\binom{[d]}{k}
and
\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}
, one has

\frac{\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})})}{\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu^{(\mathbf{S})})}=\frac{\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}}{p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}}.

Theorems
4.3
and
4.4
show that our probabilities define a probabilistic bijection. In §
5.1
, we use a generalization of Lagrange interpolation to prove Theorem
4.3
. In §
5.2
, we derive an “intrinsic” formula for the probabilities that (mostly) avoids the use of coordinates, and we use this formula to prove Theorem
4.4
.

Lemma 4.5
.

The probabilities satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)[q^{-1},t^{-1}]=\mathcal{P}_{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime}\rightarrow\nu^{\prime})[t,q],

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)[q^{-1},t^{-1}]=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime}\leftarrow\nu^{\prime})[t,q].

Proof.

Let us assume that the bottom right corner of the partitions
\lambda,\rho,\nu,\mu
in Quebecois notation has coordinates
(0,0)
, compare to Figure
5
. Then we obtain the partitions
\lambda^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}
by reflecting along the line
y=-x
, i.e., by mapping coordinates
(x,y)
to
(-y,-x)
. Because of our identification of points with coordinates
(x,y)
with monomials
q^{x}t^{y}
, this reflection corresponds to the variable transformation
q,t\rightarrow t^{-1},q^{-1}
. Hence we have by (
27
)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)[q,t]=\mathcal{P}_{\rho^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime}\rightarrow\nu^{\prime})[t^{-1},q^{-1}],

which is equivalent to the assertion. The second statement follows analogously. ∎

4.3.
Recovering dual RSK

Next we show that in the limits
q,t\rightarrow 0
and
q,t\rightarrow\infty
, our probabilistic bijection
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}
degenerates to the deterministic bijections
F^{{\rm row}}_{\lambda,\rho}
and
F^{{\rm col}}_{\lambda,\rho}
, respectively. By Lemma
4.5
and the fact that
F^{{\rm row}}_{\lambda,\rho}
is the transpose of
F^{{\rm col}}_{\lambda,\rho}
, it is enough to consider the case
q,t\rightarrow 0
.

The points
O_{j}=S_{j}
and
I_{i}=\overline{R}_{i}
satisfy

O_{0}<I_{1}<O_{1}<\cdots<I_{d}<O_{d},

where
(x,y)<(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
means that
x\leq x^{\prime},y\leq y^{\prime}
, and at least one inequality is strict. The same inequalities hold if we replace some subset of the
I_{i}
by
R_{i}
, or some subset of the
O_{j}
by
\overline{S}_{j}
. We now rewrite the definitions of
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
and
\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
by pulling out the smaller point (that is, the monomial with smaller exponents) from each binomial factor. For brevity, we write
i\not\in\mathbf{R}
and
j\not\in\mathbf{S}
instead of
i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}
and
j\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}
. The result is

(31)

p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}},\qquad\quad\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\overline{\tau}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}},

where
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
is defined as

(32)

\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S},i\not\in\mathbf{R}\atop s<i}\left(1-\frac{I_{i}}{S_{s}}\right)\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S},i\not\in\mathbf{R}\atop s\geq i}\left(1-\frac{S_{s}}{I_{i}}\right)\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R},j\not\in\mathbf{S}\atop r\leq j}\left(1-\frac{O_{j}}{R_{r}}\right)\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R},j\not\in\mathbf{S}\atop r>j}\left(1-\frac{R_{r}}{O_{j}}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S},j\not\in\mathbf{S}\atop s<j}\left(1-\frac{O_{j}}{S_{s}}\right)\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S},j\not\in\mathbf{S}\atop s>j}\left(1-\frac{S_{s}}{O_{j}}\right)\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R},i\not\in\mathbf{R}\atop r<i}\left(1-\frac{I_{i}}{R_{r}}\right)\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R},i\not\in\mathbf{R}\atop r>i}\left(1-\frac{R_{r}}{I_{i}}\right)},

(33)

\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}(S_{s})^{\#\{i\not\in\mathbf{R}\mid i>s\}-\#\{j\not\in\mathbf{S}\mid j>s\}}\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}(R_{r})^{\#\{j\not\in\mathbf{S}\mid j\geq r\}-\#\{i\not\in\mathbf{R}\mid i>r\}}\\
\times\prod_{j\not\in\mathbf{S}}(-O_{j})^{\#\{r\in\mathbf{R}\mid r>j\}-\#\{s\in\mathbf{S}\mid s>j\}}\prod_{i\not\in\mathbf{R}}(-I_{i})^{\#\{s\in\mathbf{S}\mid s\geq i\}-\#\{r\in\mathbf{R}\mid r>i\}},

and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}},\overline{\tau}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
are obtained from
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}},\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
by replacing all points
R_{r}
and
S_{s}
with
\overline{R}_{r}
and
\overline{S}_{s}
.
Each binomial factor in
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
is of the form
1-q^{x}t^{y}
for some
x,y\geq 0
, with
x,y
not both 0. It follows immediately that

(34)

\lim_{q,t\rightarrow 0}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\lim_{q,t\rightarrow 0}\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=1.

The analysis of the monomials
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
and
\overline{\tau}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
is the content of the next two lemmas.

Lemma 4.6
.

There are integers
x,y\geq 0
such that

\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\overline{\tau}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=q^{x}t^{y}.

Moreover,
x
and
y
are not both zero unless
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{R}
or
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}
.

Before proving this result, we derive some consequences.

Corollary 4.7
.

(1)

The
q,t\rightarrow 0
limit of
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}[q,t]
is the dual row insertion bijection
F_{\lambda,\rho}^{{\rm row}}
.

(2)

The
q,t\rightarrow\infty
limit of
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}[q,t]
is the dual column insertion bijection
F_{\lambda,\rho}^{{\rm col}}
.

(3)

The rational functions
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
and
\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
take values in
[0,1]
when
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows immediately from Lemma
4.6
and (
34
). Part (2) follows from part (1), Lemma
4.5
and (
17
).

For part (3), observe that by Theorem
4.3
, it suffices to show that
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
and
\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
are nonnegative when
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
. By Lemma
4.6
,
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
is nonnegative whenever
q,t\geq 0
. When
q,t\in[0,1)
, each binomial factor in
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
is strictly positive, so the result is clear. When
q,t\in(1,\infty)
, each factor in
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
is strictly negative, and the numerators and denominators of these functions contain the same number of factors.
∎

In order to prove Lemma
4.6
, we give a combinatorial interpretation to the exponents of each of the points
S_{s},R_{r},O_{j},I_{i}
appearing in
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
. We regard two complementary subsequences of
D_{0},U_{1},D_{1},\ldots,D_{d},U_{d}
:

•

The first subsequence includes all
U_{r}
with
r\in\mathbf{R}
and
D_{s}
with
s\in\mathbf{S}
.

•

The second subsequence includes all
U_{i}
with
i\notin\mathbf{R}
and
D_{j}
with
j\notin\mathbf{S}
.

We interpret both subsequences as lattice paths consisting of
up steps

(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j+1)
, and
down steps

(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j-1)
by replacing each
U
letter by an up step and each
D
letter by a down step where we fix the endpoint of both paths to lie on the
x
-axis. We label the steps by
U_{i}
or
D_{j}
respectively and denote by
u_{i}
the height of the starting point of
U_{i}
and by
d_{j}
the height of the ending point of
D_{j}
. See Figure
6
for an example.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{0}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{2}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{2}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{3}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{4}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{4}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{5}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{5}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{6}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{6}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{7}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{7}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{8}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{8}}
