Figure 6
.
The pair of paths used to compute the exponents in
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
for
\mathbf{R}=\{1,4,8\},\mathbf{S}=\{2,4,5,7\}
and
d=8
. The height sequences are
(u_{i})=(\underline{1},-2,-1,\underline{1},-1,0,0,\underline{-1})
and
(d_{i})_{i}=(-1,-2,\underline{1},-1,\underline{1},\underline{0},0,\underline{-1},0)
where the heights of steps of the first path are underlined.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
u_{i}
and
d_{j}
be defined as above, then

\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}S_{s}^{d_{s}}\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}R_{r}^{-u_{r}}\prod_{j\notin\mathbf{S}}(-O_{j})^{d_{j}}\prod_{i\notin\mathbf{R}}(-I_{i})^{-u_{i}}.

Proof.

For
s\in\mathbf{S}
, observe that the exponent of
S_{s}
in
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
is

\big|[s+1,d]\setminus\mathbf{R}\big|-\big|[s+1,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}\big|\\
=\Big(\big|[s+1,d]\big|-\big|[s+1,d]\cap\mathbf{R}\big|\Big)-\Big(\big|[s+1,d]\big|-\big|[s+1,d]\cap\mathbf{S}\big|\Big)\\
=\big|[s+1,d]\cap\mathbf{S}\big|-\big|[s+1,d]\cap\mathbf{R}\big|,

which is the difference between the number of down steps and the number of up steps occurring to the right of the down step labeled
D_{s}
. Since the path ends at height
0
, this difference is equal to
d_{s}
. The other three cases are similar.
∎

The interpretation of
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
in terms of paths is useful because it allows us to pair
R_{r}
and
S_{s}
or
I_{i}
and
O_{j}
appearing with opposite exponents. Specifically, we pair each up step above the
x
-axis with the nearest down step to its right which lies at the same height, and we pair each up step below the
x
-axis to the nearest down step to its left at the same height. Since
|\mathbf{S}|=|\mathbf{R}|
or
|\mathbf{S}|=|\mathbf{R}|+1
, it is easy to see that by this all down steps are paired except for one which ends at the
x
-axis. By identifying the up step with label
U_{i}
with
R_{i}^{-u_{i}}
if
i
is in
\mathbf{R}
and
(-I_{i})^{-u_{i}}
otherwise and the down step with label
D_{j}
with
S_{j}^{d_{j}}
of
j\in\mathbf{S}
and
(-O_{j})^{u_{j}}
otherwise, we obtain a pairing of the powers of
R_{r}
and
S_{s}
or
I_{i}
and
O_{j}
where the exponents in each pair are opposite. This implies furthermore
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\overline{\tau}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
since
\dfrac{R_{r}}{S_{s}}=\dfrac{\overline{R}_{r}}{\overline{S}_{s}}
.

Example 4.9
.

Let
d=8
and
\mathbf{R}=\{1,4,8\},\mathbf{S}=\{2,4,5,7\}
. Using the paths in Figure
6
, we find the following pairing of the powers of
R_{r}
and
S_{s}
or
I_{i}
and
O_{j}
in
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}

\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\left(\left(\dfrac{S_{2}}{R_{1}}\right)^{1}\left(\dfrac{S_{4}}{R_{4}}\right)^{1}S_{5}^{0}\left(\dfrac{S_{7}}{R_{8}}\right)^{-1}\right)\\
\times\left(\left(\dfrac{O_{0}}{I_{3}}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{O_{1}}{I_{2}}\right)^{-2}\left(\dfrac{O_{3}}{I_{5}}\right)^{-1}\left(\dfrac{O_{6}}{I_{6}}\right)^{0}\left(\dfrac{O_{8}}{I_{7}}\right)^{0}\right),

where the terms in the first bracket corresponds to the first path and the terms in the second bracket to the second path.

It is clear that each pair of up and down steps corresponds to a monomial
q^{a}t^{b}
with
a,b
nonnegative, where both
a,b
are
0
only if the end point of the down step lies on the
x
-axis. By taking the product of all pairs and the left-over down step ending on the
x
-axis, the exponents of
q
and
t
in
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
are nonnegative.
Assume that the exponent of
q
and
t
are both
0
. The steps with labels
U_{d}
and
D_{d}
must be in the same path since the down step before
U_{d}
would otherwise end at height
-1
which is a contradiction. By applying the same argument recursively for
U_{d-1},\ldots,U_{1}
, we see that the sequence of labels of the paths are
D_{0}U_{i_{1}}D_{i_{1}}\ldots U_{i_{k}}D_{i_{k}}
and
U_{j_{1}}D_{j_{1}}\ldots U_{j_{d-k}}D_{j_{d-k}}
respectively. By the definition of the paths this implies either
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{R}
or
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}
which finishes the proof of Lemma
4.6
.

4.4.
Definition and examples of
qtRSK^{*}

In this section, we introduce the probabilistic insertion algorithm
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
by interpreting the
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}
as
(q,t)
-local dual growth rules.
We freely use the definitions and notations from §
2.3
. Let
\Lambda
be a dual growth, and suppose
\square\in\Lambda
is a square in the
m\times n
grid of the form

\prec

\prec

\prec^{\prime}
\prec^{\prime}
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\nu
a

where
a\in\{0,1\}
,
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}(\lambda,\rho)
and
\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{k-a}(\lambda,\rho)
. Set

\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)=\prod_{\square\in\Lambda}\mathcal{P}(\square),\qquad\qquad\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\Lambda)=\prod_{\square\in\Lambda}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\square),

where

\mathcal{P}(\square)=\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu),\qquad\qquad\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\square)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu).

Definition 4.10
.

Suppose
A
is an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix and
P\in{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)
,
Q\in{\rm SSYT}^{*}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\subseteq(m^{n})
. Define

\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)=\sum_{\Lambda:A\rightarrow(P,Q)}\mathcal{P}(\Lambda),\qquad\qquad\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)=\sum_{\Lambda:A\rightarrow(P,Q)}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\Lambda).

We accomplish our goal of proving the dual Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials with the following theorem.

Theorem 4.11
.

The expressions
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)
define a probabilistic bijection between the weighted sets of
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrices with weight
\omega
and
\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\lambda\subseteq(m^{n})}{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)\times{\rm SSYT}^{*}(\lambda)
with weight
\overline{\omega}
, where

\omega(A)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}(x_{i}y_{j})^{A_{i,j}},\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\overline{\omega}(P,Q)=\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)
take values in
[0,1]
when
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

For a fixed
\{0,1\}
-matrix
A
, one can obtain all the dual growths associated with
A
by starting with the empty partition along the north and west boundaries, and recursively filling in the rest of the diagram.
When the top left, bottom left, and top right vertices (
\mu,\lambda,\rho
, respectively) of a square have been filled in,
\nu
is chosen according to the probability distribution
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)
.
Hence the fact that the local probabilities
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)
sum to
1
for fixed
\mu,\lambda,\rho
implies that the probabilities
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
sum to
1
for fixed
A
. Since all local probabilities take values in
[0,1]
for
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
by Corollary
4.7
(
3
), the same is true for
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
.

By starting with a fixed pair of tableaux
(P,Q)
along the right and bottom boundaries and recursively filling in the rest of the dual growth according to the local backward probabilities
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)
, one obtains that the backward probabilities
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)
sum to 1 for fixed
(P,Q)
, and take values in
[0,1]
for
q,t\in[0,1)
or
q,t\in(1,\infty)
as before.
Finally, we prove the compatibility relation in Lemma
4.12
below.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

If
\Lambda
is a dual growth from
A
to
(P,Q)
, then

\omega(A)\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\Lambda)\overline{\omega}(P,Q).

Proof.

Let
L
be a lattice path in
\Lambda
from the northeast corner
(0,n)
to the southwest corner
(n,0)
consisting of unit steps to the south or west. Let
\omega(L)
be the product of the weights of the edges in
L
, where the
i
-th vertical edge
\mu\atop{|\atop\lambda}
has weight
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)x_{i}^{|\lambda/\mu|}
, and the
(n+1-j)
-th horizontal edge
\mu-\rho
has weight
\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}(q,t)y_{j}^{|\rho/\mu|}
. Observe that the lattice path consisting of
n
south steps followed by
n
west steps has weight
\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}=\overline{\omega}(P,Q)
.

Let
\nw(L)
be the set of squares of
\Lambda
between
L
and the path consisting of
n
west steps followed by
n
south steps, and write
(i,j)\in\nw(L)
if the square in the
i
-th row from top and
j
-th column from left is in
\nw(L)
. Define a partial order on the set of lattice paths by
L\leq L^{\prime}
if
\nw(L)\subseteq\nw(L^{\prime})
. We prove by induction with respect to this partial order that

(35)

\prod_{(i,j)\in\nw(L)}(x_{i}y_{j})^{A_{i,j}}\prod_{\square\in\nw(L)}\mathcal{P}(\square)=\omega(L)\prod_{\square\in\nw(L)}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\square).

The base case is the path consisting of
n
west steps followed by
n
south steps, for which both sides of (
35
) are equal to 1. For the induction step, it suffices to consider the case where
\nw(L^{\prime})
is obtained from
\nw(L)
by adding a single square, as depicted next.

\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\nu
A_{i,j}
\varphi^{*}_{\nu/\lambda}
\psi_{\nu/\rho}
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\nu
A_{i,j}

The left hand side of (
35
) changes by the factor
(x_{i}y_{j})^{A_{i,j}}\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)
while the right hand side changes by the factor

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)\frac{\psi_{\nu/\rho}\varphi^{*}_{\nu/\lambda}}{\psi_{\lambda/\mu}\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}}x_{i}^{|\nu/\rho|-|\lambda/\mu|}y_{j}^{|\nu/\lambda|-|\rho/\mu|}.

The fact that
|\nu|-|\lambda\cup\rho|=|\lambda\cap\rho|-|\mu|+A_{i,j}
implies that the change of the exponents of
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
is equal on both sides. The equality of the remaining expressions is the statement of Theorem
4.4
.
∎

The statement of the above lemma can be reformulated as
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)=\linebreak\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\Lambda)\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t)
. It is not difficult to see, that this and Theorem
4.11
implies the more refined identities

\displaystyle\sum_{A}\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)=\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t),

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)}\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t)=1.

Remark 4.13
.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be two partitions. It is easy to see that
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
yields a proof of the skew dual Cauchy identity

(36)

\sum_{\mu}P_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\rho^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}=\sum_{\nu}P_{\nu/\rho}(\mathbf{x};q,t)P_{\nu^{\prime}/\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y};t,q).

Indeed, the left hand side of (
36
) is the weighted generating function of triples
(A,P,Q)
where
A
is an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix,
P
a skew SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
and
Q
a skew dual SSYT of shape
\rho/\mu
with respect to the weight

\omega(A,P,Q)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}(x_{i}y_{j})^{A_{i,j}}\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{Q}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}.

The right hand side of (
36
) is the weighted generating function of pairs
(\widehat{P},\widehat{Q})
where
\widehat{P}
is a skew SSYT of shape
\nu/\rho
and
\widehat{Q}
is a skew dual SSYT of shape
\nu/\lambda
with weight

\overline{\omega}(\widehat{P},\widehat{Q})=\psi_{\widehat{P}}(q,t)\varphi^{*}_{\widehat{Q}}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{\widehat{P}}\mathbf{y}^{\widehat{Q}}.

A straight forward adaption of the proof of Theorem
4.11
and of Lemma
4.12
shows that the expressions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(A,P,Q\rightarrow\widehat{P},\widehat{Q})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow\widehat{P},\widehat{Q}),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A,P,Q\leftarrow\widehat{P},\widehat{Q})

\displaystyle:=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow\widehat{P},\widehat{Q}),

yield a probabilistic bijection between the above described weighted sets.

The probabilities
\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}(A\leftarrow P,Q)
satisfy the following symmetry property which generalizes (
18
).

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
A
be an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix,
P
an SSYT and
Q
a dual SSYT of the same shape. Then

(37)

\mathcal{P}(A^{T}\rightarrow Q^{\prime},T^{\prime})[q,t]=\mathcal{P}(A\rightarrow P,Q)[t^{-1},q^{-1}],

and similarly for
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
be a dual growth from
A
to
(P,Q)
. We define
\Lambda^{\prime}
as the dual growth from
A^{T}
to
(Q^{\prime},P^{\prime})
which is obtained by transposing all partitions in
\Lambda
and then taking the transpose of the array of partitions itself. It is immediate that the map
\Lambda\mapsto\Lambda^{\prime}
is a bijection from the set of dual growths from
A
to
(P,Q)
to the set of dual growths from
A^{T}
to
(Q^{\prime},P^{\prime})
. By Lemma
4.5
we obtain for the probability of a dual growth
\Lambda^{\prime}

\mathcal{P}(\Lambda^{\prime})[q,t]=\prod_{\square^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}(\square^{\prime})[q,t]=\prod_{\square\in\Lambda}\mathcal{P}(\square)[t^{-1},q^{-1}]=\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)[t^{-1},q^{-1}].

The result follows by summing over all
\Lambda^{\prime}
.
∎

The discussion in §
2.3
, in particular Lemma
2.10
, explains how the above
(q,t)
-local dual growth rules can be translated into a probabilistic insertion algorithm. Let
T
be a semistandard Young tableau and recall that
T^{(i)}
denotes the shape of the subtableau consisting of entries at most
i
.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
T
be a semistandard Young tableau and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}
be positive integers. The
dual
(q,t)
-RSK insertion
of
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}
into
T
, denoted

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\xrightarrow{qt{\rm RSK}^{*}}T=\widehat{T},

is the probability distribution computed as follows:

•

Call the multiset
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
the
insertion queue
.

•

Let
i
be the smallest integer of the insertion queue and denote by
k
the multiplicity of
i
in the insertion queue. For each
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{k}\left(T^{(i)},\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}\right)
, place
i
in each cell of
\nu/\left(T^{(i)}\cup\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}\right)
with probability
\mathcal{P}_{T^{(i)},\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}}\left(T^{(i-1)}\rightarrow\widehat{T}^{(i)}\right)
. Delete all
i^{\prime}s
from the insertion queue and add all entries which have been replaced (bumped) by an
i
to the insertion queue.

•

Repeat the previous step until the insertion queue is empty.

Example 4.16
.

\displaystyle=qt\dfrac{(1-q)^{2}(1-t)}{(1-q^{2})(1-qt)^{2}},

where some of the probabilities were already calculated in Example
4.2
.

5.
Proof of Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
4.4

The aim of this section is to prove that the probabilities
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}
define probabilistic bijections. In §
5.1
we use a generalization of Lagrange interpolation to prove Theorem
4.3
.
In §
5.2
we define six rational functions in
q,t
, namely
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and their companions
\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta},\overline{\gamma}
and show in Proposition
5.3
an alternative definition of the probabilities using these functions. While the definition in §
4.2
allows a simple proof of Theorem
4.3
, the alternative definition given in Proposition
5.3
is analogous to the one in
[
1
]
and allows us to prove Theorem
4.4
.

5.1.
Interpolation identities

Let
\binom{A}{k}
denote the set of
k
-element subsets of
A
and write
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For an element
\mathbf{S}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}\in\binom{A}{k}
we denote by
\mathbf{x}_{S}:=(x_{s_{1}},\ldots,x_{s_{k}})
.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
6
]
)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
0
,
f\in K[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}
a symmetric function of degree at most
d
in
x_{1}
and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+d}
be pairwise different elements of
K
. Then we have

(38)

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[n+d]}{n}}f(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}})\prod_{i\in[n+d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}-a_{i})}{\prod\limits_{s\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{s}-a_{i})}.

For reasons of being self-contained, we give another proof of the above interpolation identity.

Proof.

Both the left-hand and the right-hand side of (
38
) are equal for
\mathbf{x}=(a_{s_{1}},\ldots,a_{s_{n}})
and
1\leq s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}\leq n+d
. Hence it suffices to show that these positions uniquely determine
f
. We know that
f
can be written as

f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(d)^{n}}c_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}).

The function
f
is therefore uniquely determined at the positions
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}}
for
\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[n+d]}{n}
if and only if the determinant
\det\left(s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}})\right)
, where
\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[n+d]}{n}
and
\lambda\subseteq(d)^{n}
is nonzero, i.e., the matrix
(s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}}))_{\lambda,\mathbf{S}}
is the transition matrix between the Schur polynomials and the polynomials appearing on the right hand side of (
38
). We claim that the determinant is equal to

(39)

\det\limits_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[n+d]}{n},\;\lambda\subseteq(d)^{n}}\Big(s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}})\Big)=\pm\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n+d}(a_{i}-a_{j})^{\binom{n+d-2}{n-1}}.

Note that we added
\pm
on the right-hand side of (
39
) since the sign of the determinant is depending on the actual order of the rows and columns. We prove (
39
) by using the
indication of factors
method. For
a_{i}=a_{j}
and
i\neq j
we have
\binom{n+d-2}{n-1}
pairs of rows which are identical, namely the rows of the form
\{i\}\cup\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
and
\{j\}\cup\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\subset[n+d]\setminus\{i,j\}
is a subset of size
n-1
. Hence the right hand of (
39
) side is dividing the determinant. The total-degree of the determinant is equal to the sum of
|\lambda|
, where we sum over all partitions
\lambda
included in the box
(d)^{n}
. For
\lambda=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
where we allow parts to be
0
, the total-degree of the determinant is

\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(d)^{n}}|\lambda|=\sum_{d\geq i_{1}\geq\cdots\geq i_{n}\geq 0}(i_{1}+\cdots+i_{n})=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\binom{n+d}{n+1}.

The last equality can be proven by induction on
n
. This implies that the total-degree of both sides of (
39
) coincide. Hence it suffices to prove that the leading coefficient with respect to the lexicographical order is the same on both sides. For the left-hand side we obtain the leading term by choosing for a fixed
\lambda=(d-i_{1},\ldots,d-i_{n})
the set
\mathbf{S}=\{1+i_{1},2+i_{2},\ldots,n+i_{n}\}
and then the monomial corresponding to the semistandard Young tableau with the
j
-th row filled by
j+i_{j}
. It is easy to see that this yields the leading coefficient and is, up to sign, equal to
1
. Since the leading coefficient of the right-hand side is
1
, this proves the assertion.
∎

For
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}
and two sequences
\textbf{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}),\textbf{b}=(b_{0},\ldots,b_{d})
of pairwise different variables, define

p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})=\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{j}-b_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{R}}(b_{j}-b_{i})}\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{R}}(b_{j}-a_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{j}-a_{i})}.

Note that
p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})=p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{R}}(\textbf{b},\textbf{a})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For fixed
\mathbf{R}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}
, we have

(40)

\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}}p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})=1.

By the symmetry of
p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
in
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{S}
, we also have

(41)

\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}}p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}(\textbf{b},\textbf{a})=1,

for fixed
\mathbf{S}
.

Proof.

Define the symmetric function

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\;\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(x_{j}-b_{i}).

Since
f
has degree
d+1-k
in
x_{1}
, Lemma
5.1
implies

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}}f(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{S}})\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}(x_{j}-a_{i})}{\prod\limits_{s\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{s}-a_{i})}.

By evaluating
f
at
(b_{r_{1}},\ldots,b_{r_{k}})
, where
\mathbf{R}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\}
, we obtain

\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\;\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(b_{r}-b_{i})=\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\binom{[0,d]}{k}}\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}\;\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}(a_{s}-b_{i})\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{r\in\mathbf{R}}(b_{r}-a_{i})}{\prod\limits_{s\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{s}-a_{i})}.

Dividing by the left-hand side, we obtain the assertion.
∎

The last ingredient for the proof of Theorem
4.3
is the following limit which can be verified easily

(42)

\lim_{b_{0}\rightarrow\infty}p^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})=\prod_{i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{j}-b_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{R}\cap[d]}(b_{j}-b_{i})}\prod_{i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{R}\cap[d]}(b_{j}-a_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}}(a_{j}-a_{i})}.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

Lemma
5.2
and (
42
) imply equation (
29
) by setting
a_{i}=O_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq d
and
b_{i}=R_{i}
for
i\in\mathbf{R}
and
b_{i}=I_{i}
for
i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}
.
Analogously equation (
30
) is obtained from (
41
) together with (
42
) by setting
a_{i}=\overline{S}_{i}
for
i\in\mathbf{S}
and
a_{i}=O_{i}
for
i\in[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}
, and
b_{i}=I_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
.
∎

5.2.
Probabilities via
(q,t)
-hook-lengths

By definition, we have

(43)

\dfrac{\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)}{\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)}=\dfrac{\psi_{\lambda/\mu}\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}}{\psi_{\nu/\rho}\varphi^{*}_{\nu/\lambda}}\\
=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}}\dfrac{b_{\mu}(c)}{b_{\lambda}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\rho/\mu}}\dfrac{b_{\rho}(c)}{b_{\mu}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\rho}-\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\rho}}\dfrac{b_{\nu}(c)}{b_{\rho}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda}-\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda}}\dfrac{b_{\lambda}(c)}{b_{\nu}(c)}.

In §
5.3
, we analyze the contribution of each cell
c\in\nu
to the right-hand side of (
43
) to obtain the alternative formula

(44)

\dfrac{\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)}{\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)}=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\lambda\cap\rho}}\dfrac{b_{\mu}(c)}{b_{\rho}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\rho/\lambda\cap\rho}}\\
\times\dfrac{b_{\lambda}(c)}{b_{\mu}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\rho}}\dfrac{b_{\nu}(c)}{b_{\lambda}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\lambda}}\dfrac{b_{\rho}(c)}{b_{\nu}(c)}.

In Proposition
5.3
we provide an alternative formula for the fraction of the probabilities

(45)

\dfrac{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)}{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)}=\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\overline{\beta}_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}}{\alpha_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\beta_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}},

where the terms on the right-hand side are defined below. Furthermore Proposition
5.3
implies that the right-hand side of (
44
) is equal to the right-hand side of (
45
).
Thus, Theorem
4.4
follows from Proposition
5.3
and (
44
).

Recall that by Lemma
4.6
, we have
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
and
\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
, where
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
is a monomial in
q
and
t
with nonnegative exponents, and
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}},\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
are ratios of products of binomials of the form
1-q^{x}t^{y}
, with
x,y\geq 0
. These binomial factors are in fact
(q,t)
-hook-lengths of cells in
\lambda,\rho,\mu^{(\mathbf{R})},
and
\nu^{(\mathbf{S})}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mu=\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}
and
\nu=\nu^{(\mathbf{S})}
. The probabilities
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)
and
\overline{p}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)
are given by the formulas

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)=\tau_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}\dfrac{\alpha_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\beta_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}}{\gamma_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}},\qquad\quad\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\leftarrow\nu)=\tau_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\overline{\beta}_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}}{\gamma_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}},

where
\tau_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}=\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
,

\alpha_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)}{h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\lambda}}\dfrac{h_{\rho}^{a}(c)}{h_{\nu}^{a}(c)},

\beta_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\lambda\cap\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\rho}^{\ell}(c)}{h_{\mu}^{\ell}(c)}\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\rho/\lambda\cap\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)}{h_{\mu}^{a}(c)},

\gamma_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}=\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\rho}^{\ell}(c)h_{\rho}^{a}(c)}{\displaystyle\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}}h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c)h_{\nu}^{a}(c)\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\mu}^{\ell}(c)h_{\mu}^{a}(c)},

and
\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,\rho,\nu},\overline{\beta}_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}
are defined in the same way as
\alpha_{\lambda,\rho,\nu},\beta_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}
, but with all the
h_{\kappa}^{\ell}
’s and
h_{\kappa}^{a}
’s interchanged.

Recall the rational functions
\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
defined in (
32
).
Lemma
5.4
implies

\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\dfrac{\alpha_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\beta_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}}{\gamma_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}},

and Lemma
5.5
implies

\overline{\zeta}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,\rho,\nu}\overline{\beta}_{\mu,\lambda,\rho}}{\gamma_{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu}}.

Hence Proposition
5.3
is a direct consequence of these lemmas which we prove next.

Lemma 5.4
.

We have

(46)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)}{h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c)}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{i<j\leq d}\left(1-\dfrac{I_{j}}{S_{i}}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{i<j\leq d\atop j\not\in\mathbf{S}}\left(1-\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}\right)\prod_{i\leq j\leq d\atop j\in\mathbf{S}}\left(1-t\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}\right)},

(47)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}-\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cup\rho/\lambda}}\dfrac{h_{\rho}^{a}(c)}{h_{\nu}^{a}(c)}=\prod_{j\in\mathbf{S}}\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i\leq j}\left(1-\dfrac{S_{j}}{I_{i}}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{0\leq i<j\atop i\not\in\mathbf{S}}\left(1-\dfrac{S_{j}}{O_{i}}\right)\prod_{0\leq i\leq j\atop i\in\mathbf{S}}\left(1-q\dfrac{S_{j}}{O_{i}}\right)},

(48)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\lambda\cap\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\rho}^{\ell}(c)}{h_{\mu}^{\ell}(c)}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{R}}\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j\leq d}\left(1-\dfrac{O_{j}}{R_{i}}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{i<j\leq d\atop j\not\in\mathbf{R}}\left(1-\dfrac{I_{j}}{R_{i}}\right)\prod_{i<j\leq d\atop j\in\mathbf{R}}\left(1-t^{-1}\dfrac{I_{j}}{R_{i}}\right)},

(49)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{R}_{\rho/\lambda\cap\rho}}\dfrac{h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)}{h_{\mu}^{a}(c)}=\prod_{j\in\mathbf{R}}\dfrac{\displaystyle\prod_{0\leq i<j}\left(1-\dfrac{R_{j}}{O_{i}}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\atop i\not\in\mathbf{R}}\left(1-\dfrac{R_{j}}{I_{i}}\right)\prod_{1\leq i<j\atop i\in\mathbf{R}}\left(1-q^{-1}\dfrac{R_{j}}{I_{i}}\right)},

(50)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}}h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c)h_{\nu}^{a}(c)=\prod_{i,j\in\mathbf{S}\atop i\leq j}\left(1-t\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}\right)\left(1-q\dfrac{S_{j}}{O_{i}}\right),

(51)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\mu}^{\ell}(c)h_{\mu}^{a}(c)=\prod_{i,j\in\mathbf{R}\atop i<j}\left(1-q^{-1}\dfrac{R_{j}}{I_{i}}\right)\left(1-t^{-1}\dfrac{I_{j}}{R_{i}}\right),

(52)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c)h_{\lambda}^{a}(c)=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{S},j\in\mathbf{R}\atop i<j}\left(1-\dfrac{I_{j}}{S_{i}}\right)\left(1-\dfrac{R_{j}}{O_{i}}\right),

(53)

\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda\cup\rho}\cap\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}}h_{\rho}^{\ell}(c)h_{\rho}^{a}(c)=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{R},j\in\mathbf{S}\atop i\leq j}\left(1-\dfrac{O_{j}}{R_{i}}\right)\left(1-\dfrac{S_{j}}{I_{i}}\right).

Proof.

We prove the equations (
46
) and (
50
), the proofs of the other equations are analogue.

Let
i\in\mathbf{S}
and
i<j\leq d
. We consider the cells
c_{i,j}^{(1)},\ldots,c_{i,j}^{(m)}
of
\nu
in the same row as the
i
-th addable outer corner where the left corner of
c_{i,j}^{(1)}
is below
I_{j}
and the right corner of
c_{i,j}^{(m)}
is below
O_{j}
, see Figure
7
.

.
Two schematic situations for the cells

.

We have
h_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(k+1)}\right)=h_{\nu}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(k)}\right)
for all
1\leq k<m
and

h_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(1)}\right)=1-\frac{I_{j}}{S_{i}},\qquad h_{\nu}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(m)}\right)=\begin{cases}1-\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}\qquad&j\notin\mathbf{S},\\[11.0pt]
1-t\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{j}}&j\in\mathbf{S}.\end{cases}

Hence the total contribution of the cells
c_{i,j}^{(1)},\ldots,c_{i,j}^{(m)}
is

\prod_{k=1}^{m}\frac{h_{\lambda}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(k)}\right)}{h_{\nu}^{\ell}\left(c_{i,j}^{(k)}\right)}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1-\frac{I_{j}}{S_{i}}}{1-\frac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}}\qquad&j\notin\mathbf{S},\\[18.0pt]
\dfrac{1-\frac{I_{j}}{S_{i}}}{1-t\frac{O_{j}}{S_{i}}}&j\in\mathbf{S}.\end{cases}

For

let

denote the
i
-th addable outer corner. By definition we have
h_{\lambda}^{\ell}(c_{i})=1
and
h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c_{i})=1-t=1-t\frac{O_{i}}{S_{i}}
.
The left hand side of (
46
) is the product over the cells
c_{i,j}^{(1)},\ldots,c_{i,j}^{(m)}
of the above form for all

and
i<j\leq d
and all added outer corners
c_{i}
with
i\in\mathbf{S}
. Combining the above we therefore obtain the right hand side of (
46
).

the unique cell that lies in the same row as the
i
-th addable outer corner and in the same column as the
j
-th addable outer corner for
i\leq j
as shown in Figure
8
.
It is immediate that the
(q,t)
-hook lengths of
c_{i,j}
with respect to
\nu
are

h_{\nu}^{\ell}(c_{i,j})=1-t\dfrac{O_{j}}{S_{i}},\qquad\qquad h_{\nu}^{a}(c_{i,j})=1-q\dfrac{S_{j}}{O_{i}}.

Since the cells contributing to the product on the left hand side of (
50
) are exactly all
c_{i,j}
with
i,j\in\mathbf{S}
, this implies the assertion.∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Interchanging all
h^{\ell}
and

on the left hand side of the equations (

)-(

) has the effect of replacing

and

by
\overline{S}_{j}
for all
1\leq i\leq d
and
0\leq j\leq d
on the right hand side of the equations.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Table 3
.
The color and shading code for marking the cells of the skew shapes
\lambda/(\lambda\cap\rho),\rho/(\lambda\cap\rho),(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu,
and
\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)
.

Let
c
be a cell of
\nu
, and define
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)
to be the contribution of
c
to the right-hand side of (
43
). Let
R_{c}
(resp.,
C_{c}
) be the subset of skew shapes
\kappa\in\{\lambda,\mu,-,+\}
such that
c
lies in the same row (resp., column) as a cell of
\kappa
. We call
R_{c}
(resp.,
C_{c}
) the
row type
(resp.,
column type
) of
c
, and define the
type
of
c
to be the pair
(R_{c},C_{c})
. Since we assume that
\lambda/\mu,\nu/\rho
are horizontal strips and
\rho/\mu,\nu/\lambda
are vertical strips, the possible row types are either
R_{c}=\{\rho\}
or
R_{c}\subseteq\{\lambda,+,-\}
. Similarly, the possible column types are either
C_{c}=\{\lambda\}
or
C_{c}\subseteq\{\rho,-,+\}
. It is immediate that
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)
is defined piecewise depending on the row and column type of
c
. It follows from (
43
) that
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)=1
if
R_{c}=\{\rho\}
or
C_{c}=\{\lambda\}
, hence we ignore these two cases for now. Table
4
contains formulas for the other cases where we abbreviate
b_{\kappa}(c)
by
\kappa
for reasons of readability.

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc|cccc}\text{\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\;R_{c}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$C_{c}$}}}}&\emptyset&\{-\}&\{+\}&\{-,+\}&\{\rho\}&\{\rho,-\}&\{\rho,+\}&\{\rho,-,+\}\\
\hline\cr&&&&&\\[-8.0pt]
\emptyset&1&\frac{\lambda}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda\,\rho}{\mu\,\nu}&1&\frac{\lambda}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda\,\rho}{\mu\,\nu}\\[10.0pt]
\{-\}&\frac{\mu}{\rho}&\frac{\lambda}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda}{\nu}&\frac{\mu}{\rho}&\frac{\lambda}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda}{\nu}\\[10.0pt]
\{+\}&\frac{\nu}{\lambda}&\frac{\nu}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\lambda}&\frac{\rho}{\mu}&\frac{\nu}{\lambda}&\frac{\nu}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\lambda}&\frac{\rho}{\mu}\\[10.0pt]
\{-,+\}&\frac{\mu\,\nu}{\lambda\,\rho}&\frac{\nu}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\lambda}&1&\frac{\mu\,\nu}{\lambda\,\rho}&\frac{\nu}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\lambda}&1\\[8.0pt]
\hline\cr&&&&\\[-8.0pt]
\{\lambda\}&1&\frac{\lambda}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda\,\rho}{\mu\,\nu}&1&\frac{\lambda}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda\,\rho}{\mu\,\nu}\\[10.0pt]
\{\lambda,-\}&\frac{\mu}{\rho}&\frac{\lambda}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda}{\nu}&\frac{\mu}{\rho}&\frac{\lambda}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\nu}&\frac{\lambda}{\nu}\\[10.0pt]
\{\lambda,+\}&\frac{\nu}{\lambda}&\frac{\nu}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\lambda}&\frac{\rho}{\mu}&\frac{\nu}{\lambda}&\frac{\nu}{\mu}&\frac{\rho}{\lambda}&\frac{\rho}{\mu}\\[10.0pt]
\{\lambda,-,+\}&\frac{\mu\,\nu}{\lambda\,\rho}&\frac{\nu}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\lambda}&1&\frac{\mu\,\nu}{\lambda\,\rho}&\frac{\nu}{\rho}&\frac{\mu}{\lambda}&1\end{array}

Table 4
.
Explicit formulas for
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)
depending on the type of
c
.

We prove the formula for the second entry in the first row; the other formulas are obtained in a similar manner. Let
c
be a cell with type
(\emptyset,\{-\})
. This implies that
c
is an element of
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\rho/\mu}
but not of
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda/\mu}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\rho/\mu}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\lambda}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\nu/\rho}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\lambda}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\nu/\rho}
. Hence we have

\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)=\frac{b_{\rho}(c)}{b_{\mu}(c)}.

The type
(\emptyset,\{-\})
of
c
implies
a_{\rho}(c)=a_{\lambda}(c)
and
\ell_{\rho}(c)=\ell_{\lambda}(c)
and hence
b_{\rho}(c)=b_{\lambda}(c)
, which proves the formula for the second entry in the first row.

For
R\subseteq\{\lambda,-,+\}
and
C\subseteq\{\rho,-,+\}
, denote by
[R,C](c)
the expression for
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)
given by row
R
and column
C
of Table
4
. Observe that the four quadrants of Table
4
are identical, i.e., for all
R,C\subseteq\{-,+\}
one has

(54)

[R,C](c)=[R\cup\{\lambda\},C](c)=[R,C\cup\{\rho\}](c)=[R\cup\{\lambda\},C\cup\{\rho\}](c).

Furthermore, for
R,C\subseteq\{-,+\}
one has

(55)

[R,C](c)=\prod_{\kappa\in R}[\{\kappa\},\emptyset](c)\prod_{\kappa\in C}[\emptyset,\{\kappa\}](c).

Using these observations, together with the fact that
\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)=1
if
R_{c}=\{\rho\}
or
C_{c}=\{\lambda\}
, we obtain the formula

\prod_{c\in\nu}\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)=\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}R_{c}\neq\{\rho\}\\
C_{c}\neq\{\lambda\}\end{subarray}}\omega_{\lambda,\rho}^{\mu,\nu}(c)=\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}R_{c}\neq\{\rho\}\\
C_{c}\neq\{\lambda\}\end{subarray}}[R_{c}\cap\{-,+\},C_{c}\cap\{-,+\}](c)\\
=\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\{-\}\subseteq R_{c}\\
C_{c}\neq\{\lambda\}\end{subarray}}[\{-\},\emptyset](c)\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\{-\}\subseteq C_{c}\\
R_{c}\neq\{\rho\}\end{subarray}}[\emptyset,\{-\}](c)\\
\times\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\{+\}\subseteq R_{c}\\
C_{c}\neq\{\lambda\}\end{subarray}}[\{+\},\emptyset](c)\prod_{c:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\{+\}\subseteq C_{c}\\
R_{c}\neq\{\rho\}\end{subarray}}[\emptyset,\{+\}](c).

By the definition of the type of a cell, the condition for
c
in the first product above is equivalent to
c\in\mathcal{R}_{\lambda\cap\rho/\mu}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\lambda\cap\rho}
. Since
[\{-\},\emptyset](c)=\frac{b_{\mu}(c)}{b_{\rho}(c)}
, the first product in the above expression is equal to the first product of (
44
). By analogous arguments for the three other products, we see that the above is equal to (
44
).

6.
Degenerations

6.1.
q
-Whittaker and Hall–Littlewood degenerations

In this section we study the
t=0
(
q
-Whittaker dual row insertion) and
q=0
(Hall–Littlewood dual row insertion) degenerations of the probabilities
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu^{(\mathbf{R})},\nu^{(\mathbf{S})})[q,t]=p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,t]
. By Lemma
4.5
these two specializations can be translated to the
t\rightarrow\infty
(
q
-Whittaker dual column insertion) and
q\rightarrow\infty
(Hall–Littlewood dual column insertion) degenerations. The key to understanding these degenerations is the combinatorial description of the exponents of
q
and
t
in the monomial
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
presented in §
4.3
.
The notation
\shift(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}):=\{i:|\mathbf{R}\cap[i+1,d]|>|\mathbf{S}\cap[i+1,d]|\}
turns out useful for the next two lemmas.

Lemma 6.1

(
q
-Whittaker specialization)
.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be two partitions,
d
the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\mu
and
\mu=\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}
. Denote by
\alpha\subseteq[0,d]
the set of integers
i
with
(i+1)\in\mathbf{R}
such that the
i
-th addable outer corner is in the same row as the
(i+1)
-st removable inner corner, i.e.,
q^{x}S_{i}=R_{i+1}
for some
x\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,0]=0
unless
\shift(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S})\subseteq\alpha\cup\{0\}
, in which case we have

(56)

p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[q,0]=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{S}\setminus\left(\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\right)}\left(1-\dfrac{S_{i}}{I_{i}}\right)\prod\limits_{i\in\alpha\setminus\mathbf{S}}\left(1-\dfrac{R_{i+1}}{O_{i}}\right)}{\prod\limits_{i\in\alpha\setminus\left(\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\right)}\left(1-\dfrac{R_{i+1}}{I_{i}}\right)}\prod_{i\in\shift(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S})}\frac{R_{i+1}}{S_{i}}.

Proof.

We use the description
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}=\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
in (
31
) to calculate the probability for
t=0
. Analogously to (
34
), it is immediate that
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\neq 0
. Therefore
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
determines if the probability is equal to
0
or not.
Analogously to Lemma
4.8
and the discussion thereafter, we calculate
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
by constructing two paths and pairing all up steps and down steps of each path with the exception of one down step.
Define
p:[d]\rightarrow[0,d]
as the injection such that
U_{i}
is paired with
D_{p(i)}
for
i\in[d]
and by
c_{i}
the contribution of the pair
(U_{i},D_{p(i)})
to
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
.
Then
c_{i}=\left(\frac{S_{p(i)}}{R_{i}}\right)^{z}
if
i\in\mathbf{R}
and
c_{i}=\left(\frac{O_{p(i)}}{I_{i}}\right)^{z}
if
i\in[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}
, where
z
is a certain integer satisfying

z=\begin{cases}\text{positive}\quad&U_{i}\text{ is strictly above the $x$-axis (this implies $p(i)\geq i$)},\\
0&\text{the starting point of $U_{i}$ is on the $x$-axis},\\
\text{positive}\quad&U_{i}\text{ is weakly below the $x$-axis (this implies $p(i)<i$)}.\end{cases}

It is immediate that the exponent of
t
in
c_{i}
is positive for
i\in\mathbf{R}
unless
U_{i}
starts on the
x
-axis, or
p(i)=i-1
and
\frac{R_{i}}{S_{i-1}}
is a power of
q
, i.e.,
(i-1)\in\alpha\cap\mathbf{S}
. This implies inductively, starting with the largest
i\in\mathbf{R}
, that
\left.c_{i}\right|_{t=0}\neq 0
for all
i\in\mathbf{R}
if and only if
p(i)\in\{i,i-1\}
for all
i\in\mathbf{R}
and
p(i)=i
if
(i-1)\notin\alpha
. As a consequence, the second path consists of alternating up and down steps where all up steps start at the
x
-axis, implying the first part of the assertion.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{0}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{1}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{2}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{3}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{4}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{5}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{6}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{7}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{8}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{1}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{3}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{4}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{5}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{6}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{7}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U_{8}}

D_{1}

Figure 9
.
An example of the map
p
restricted to
\mathbf{R}=\{2,4,5,8\}
where
\alpha=\{1,3,4\}
together with the corresponding paths. The dotted lines denote the alternative choices for
p(2),p(4)
and
p(5)
which preserve
\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{t=0}\neq 0
.

Assume
\mathbf{S}
is chosen such that
\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{t=0}\neq 0
. By the above considerations it is immediate that
\left.c_{i}\right|_{t=0}=\frac{R_{i}}{S_{i-1}}
if
p(i)=i-1
, i.e.,
(i-1)\in\shift(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S})
, and
\left.c_{i}\right|_{t=0}=1
otherwise. Hence we obtain

\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{t=0}=\prod_{i\in\shift(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S})}\frac{R_{i+1}}{S_{i}}.

Finally, observe that all fractions in the products of (
32
) have positive powers of
t
except the term
\frac{S_{i}}{I_{i}}
for
i\in\mathbf{S}\setminus\left(\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\right)
, the fraction
\frac{R_{i+1}}{O_{i}}
for
i\in\alpha\setminus\mathbf{S}
and
\frac{R_{i+1}}{I_{i}}
for
i\in\alpha\setminus\left(\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\right)
, which proves the assertion.
∎

By the above lemma and Definition
4.15
, we can describe the

q
-Whittaker dual row insertion
as follows.
Let
T
be an SSYT and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n}
be positive integers which we want to insert simultaneously into
T
.
For the
k
-th step of the insertion algorithm let
\mu=\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}=T^{(k-1)},\lambda=T^{(k)}
and denote by
\rho
the shape of the SSYT obtained in the previous step restricted to the entries at most
k-1
. Denote by
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}
the addable outer cells of
\lambda\cup\rho
and define
\alpha
as the set of indices
j
such that
(j+1)\in\mathbf{R}
and
a_{j}
is in the same row as
c_{j+1}
. For simplicity, we use the notation

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\qquad&\text{if }k\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\},\\
\mathbf{R}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We (re-)insert an entry
k
into
T
for each
j\in\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
, starting with the smallest
j
, according to the following rule.

•

If
(j-1)\notin\alpha
or if there is already an entry
k
in the box
a_{j-1}
, (re-)insert
k
into the box
a_{j}
with probability
1
.

•

If
(j-1)\in\alpha
and there is no entry
k
in the box
a_{j-1}
, reinsert
k
into the box
a_{j-1}
with probability
\frac{R_{j}}{S_{j-1}}
if
(j-1)\in\mathbf{R}
and with probability
\frac{R_{j}}{S_{j-1}}\left(1-\frac{S_{j-1}}{I_{j-1}}\right)
otherwise; and reinsert
k
into
a_{j}
with probability
\left(1-\frac{R_{j}}{O_{j-1}}\right)
.

•

If
k
is placed into a cell
a_{j}
with an entry
z
in
T
, then
z
is bumped and reinserted in the
z
-th insertion step.

Finally, we have to multiply all individual probabilities from above and divide the result by
\prod_{j\in\alpha\setminus\left(\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}\right)}\left(1-\frac{R_{j+1}}{I_{j}}\right)
.

Remark 6.2
.

The
q
-Whittaker dual row insertion was first described by Matveev and Petrov
[
13
, §5.1]
for Gelfand Tsetlin patterns which are known to be in bijection to semistandard Young tableaux, see for example

[
20
, p. 313]
.

Next we describe our probabilities for
q=0
. For readability, we write
\mathbf{R}^{c}=[d]\setminus\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{S}^{c}=[0,d]\setminus\mathbf{S}
.

Lemma 6.3

(Hall–Littlewood specialization)
.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be two partitions,
d
the number of removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
and
\mu=\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}
. We define
\beta\subseteq[0,d]
as the set of integers
i
such that the
i
-th addable outer corner is in the column next to the
(i+1)
-st removable inner corner, i.e.,
t^{x}O_{i}=I_{i+1}
for a suitable
x\in\mathbb{N}
and
(i+1)\in\mathbf{R}^{c}
.
Then
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[0,t]=0
unless
\shift(\mathbf{R}^{c},\mathbf{S}^{c})\subseteq\beta
in which case we have

(57)

p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[0,t]=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{S}\cap\beta}\left(1-\dfrac{I_{i+1}}{S_{i}}\right)\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{R}\setminus\mathbf{S}}\left(1-\dfrac{O_{i}}{R_{i}}\right)}{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{R}\cap\beta}\left(1-\dfrac{I_{i+1}}{R_{i}}\right)}\prod_{i\in\shift(\mathbf{R}^{c},\mathbf{S}^{c})}\frac{I_{i+1}}{O_{i}}.

Proof.

It is immediate, that
\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\zeta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\neq 0
, hence
\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{q=0}
determines if
p_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}[0,t]
is nonzero.
Analogously to the proof of Lemma
6.1
we denote by
p:[d]\rightarrow[0,d]
the pairing of up and down steps and by
c_{i}
the contribution of the pair
(U_{i},D_{p(i)})
to
\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}
. For
i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}
it follows that the exponent of
q
in
c_{i}
is positive unless
U_{i}
starts on the
x
-axis or
p(i)=i-1
and
O_{i-1}t^{x}=I_{i}
, i.e.,
(i-1)\in\beta
. This implies inductively, starting with the largest
i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}
, that
\left.c_{i}\right|_{q=0}\neq 0
for all
i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}
if and only if
p(i)\in\{i,i-1\}
for all
i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}
and
p(i)=i
if
(i-1)\notin\beta
. See Figure
10
for an example. As a consequence the first path consists in this case alternately of up and down steps and we obtain

\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{q=0}=\prod_{i\in\shift\left(\mathbf{R}^{c},\mathbf{S}^{c}\right)}\frac{I_{i+1}}{O_{i}}.

\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{2}}
\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{3}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{4}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D_{5}}

Figure 10
.
An example of
p
restricted to
\mathbf{R}^{c}=\{1,3,4,6,7,8\}
where
\beta=\{0,2,5,6,7\}
together with the corresponding paths. The dotted lines denote the alternative choices for
p(1),p(3),p(6),p(7)
and
p(8)
which preserve that
\left.\tau_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{S}}\right|_{q=0}
is nonzero.

We observe that all fractions in the products of (
32
) have positive powers in
q
except for

\frac{I_{i+1}}{S_{i}}
for
i\in\mathbf{S}\cap\beta
, the fraction
\frac{O_{i}}{R_{i}}
for
i\in\mathbf{R}\setminus\mathbf{S}
and
\frac{I_{i+1}}{R_{i}}
for
i\in\mathbf{R}\cap\beta
which proves the claim.
∎

We translate the dual growth rules for Hall–Littlewood polynomials into a
Hall–Littlewood dual row insertion
by Definition
4.15
. Let
T
be an SSYT and
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n}
be positive integers which we want to insert simultaneously into
T
. For the
k
-th step of the insertion algorithm let
\mu=\mu^{(\mathbf{R})}=T^{(k-1)},\lambda=T^{(k)}
and denote by
\rho
be the shape of the SSYT obtained in the previous step restricted to entries at most
k-1
. Let
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}
the addable outer corners of
\lambda\cup\rho
and define
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
as before. We (re-)insert
k
for each
j\in\widehat{R}
, starting with the smallest
j
, according to the following rules.

•

For
j\notin\beta
or
j+1\in\mathbf{R}
we (re-)insert
k
into the cell
a_{j}
with probability
1
.

•

If
j\in\beta
we (re-)insert
k
into a box
a_{i}
, where
j\leq i
satisfies
\{j,j+1,\ldots,i-1\}\subseteq\beta
, with probability

\left(1-\dfrac{I_{i+1}}{S_{i}}\right)^{[i\in\beta]}\left(1-\dfrac{O_{j}}{R_{j}}\right)^{[i\neq j]}\left(1-\frac{I_{j+1}}{R_{j}}\right)^{-1}\prod\limits_{l=j}^{i-1}\dfrac{I_{l+1}}{O_{l}},

where
[\text{expression}]
is the Iverson bracket which is
1
if ‘
expression
’ is true and
0
otherwise, and using the convention
\frac{O_{0}}{R_{0}}=\frac{I_{1}}{R_{0}}=0
.

•

If
k
is placed into a cell
a_{i}
with an entry
z
in
T
, then
z
is bumped and reinserted in the
z
-th insertion step.

Remark 6.4
.

By Lemma
4.5
and (
18
), one can define the

q
-Whittaker dual column insertion
as the process obtained by first conjugating all objects, applying the Hall–Littlewood dual-row insertion and finally replacing
t
by
q^{-1}
. The
q
-Whittaker dual column insertion was first introduced by Matveev and Petrov in
[
13
, §5.4]
for Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns. A Hall–Littlewood dual column insertion can be defined analogously.

6.2.
Jack limit

The Jack polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x;\alpha)
are a one-parameter family of symmetric functions introduced by Henry Jack
[
10
]
. They are obtained from the Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x;q,t)
by setting
q=t^{\alpha}
, and taking the limit
t\rightarrow 1
. The next theorem states an intriguing property for the
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
correspondence when restricting to the Jack polynomial setting. It can be seen easily that it does not hold in the general Macdonald setting.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
A
be an
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrix such that at most one entry in each column is nonzero. In the Jack limit of
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
, interchanging adjacent columns of
A
does not affect the distribution of the
P
-tableaux.

As shown in the next section, restricting to
\{0,1\}
-matrix with at most one nonzero entry per column yields a
(q,t)
-variation of RS for words. The above theorem and Lemma
4.5
imply that interchanging two adjacent rows, when restricted to
\{0,1\}
-matrices with at most one nonzero entry per row, does not affect the distribution of the
Q
-tableaux.
Before proving the above theorem we show that it can not be extended to all
\{0,1\}
-matrices. Regard the matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

One can check easily that the tableau

The probability of the first dual growth is

\mathcal{P}_{(1),(1)}\bigl(\emptyset\rightarrow(2)\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{(2),(1)}\bigl((1)\rightarrow(2,1)\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{(2,1),(2,1)}\bigl((2)\rightarrow(2,2)\bigr)\\
=\frac{q(1-t)}{1-qt}\cdot\frac{qt(1-q)}{1-q^{2}t}\cdot\frac{q(1-t)(1-q^{2}t)}{(1+q)(1-qt)^{2}}=\frac{q^{3}t(1-q)(1-t)^{2}}{(1+q)(1-qt)^{3}},

and for the second growth

\mathcal{P}_{(1),(1)}\bigl(\emptyset\rightarrow(1,1)\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{(1),(1,1)}\bigl((1)\rightarrow(2,1)\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{(2,1),(2,1)}\bigl((1,1)\rightarrow(2,2)\bigr)\\
=\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\cdot\frac{1-t}{1-qt^{2}}\cdot\frac{(1-q)(1-qt^{2})}{(1+t)(1-qt)^{2}}=\frac{(1-q)^{2}(1-t)}{(1+t)(1-qt)^{3}}.

By summing both probabilities and taking the Jack limit, we obtain

\frac{\alpha}{2\alpha(1+\alpha)^{2}},

which is not
0
and hence different to the probability of obtaining
T
as the
P
-tableau for
A
.

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

Let
A^{\prime}
be the matrix obtained from
A
by interchanging the
k
-th and
(k+1)
-st columns. The assertion is immediate if at most one of these columns has a nonzero entry. Denote by
i_{0}<j_{0}
the rows of the
1
entry of the
k
-th or
(k+1)
-st column respectively.
Define
M=M_{k}(A)
as the number of
1
entries strictly below and to the left of the entry
(j_{0},k)
. We prove the assertion by induction on
M
. We postpone the proof of the base case and assume that we proved the statement for
M-1
. Let
l<k
be maximal such that an entry in column
l
and a row below
j_{0}
is equal to
1
. By the induction hypothesis we can consecutively interchange the columns
(l,l+1),(l+1,l+2),\ldots,(k,k+1)
which has the effect of putting the
l
-th column to the right of former
(k+1)
-st column and reducing
M_{k}(A)
by 1. We can now interchange the former
k
-th and
(k+1)
-st column by the induction hypothesis and then consecutively interchange columns to move the former
l
-th column back to its original position. Hence we realized interchanging the columns
k,k+1
by a series of interchanges where the corresponding
M_{i}
is at most
M-1
. We can therefore restrict ourselves to the base case.

In the following we regard dual growths associated with
A
and
A^{\prime}
restricted to the
k
-th and
(k+1)
-st columns. We denote by I the case in which the 1 entries are in positions
(i_{0},k)
and
(j_{0},k+1)
and by II the case with 1 entries in positions
(i_{0},k+1)
and
(j_{0},k)
, compare to Figure
11
.

\mu_{0}
\mu_{0}
\mu_{0}
\mu_{0}
\rho_{0}
\rho_{0}

\mu_{1}
\rho_{1}

X

.
A schematic of dual growth diagrams associated with

and

We claim that the probability to obtain the partitions
\mu,\rho,\rho
at the top of row
j_{0}
for a growth diagram in case I is equal to the probability of obtaining the partitions
\mu,\mu,\rho
in case II. It is immediate that each square above row
i_{0}
has probability
1
in both cases. For row
i_{0}
the probabilities of the squares are
\mathcal{P}_{\mu_{0},\mu_{0}}(\mu_{0}\rightarrow\rho_{0})
and
1
in situation I and
1
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mu_{0},\mu_{0}}(\mu_{0}\rightarrow\rho_{0})
in situation II. Similarly for the rows between
i_{0}
and
j_{0}
, the probabilities for the squares in case I and II are both
\mathcal{P}_{\mu_{i+1},\rho_{i}}(\mu_{i}\rightarrow\rho_{i+1})
and
1
, however in different order, which proves the claim.

Finally, since we can assume
A_{i,j}=0
for all
i>j_{0}
and
j<k
, the sequences of partitions for all rows below row
j_{0}
are for both case I and II of the form
\lambda,*,\nu
; the probabilities of the corresponding squares are therefore always
1
.
Hence it suffices to show that for all partitions
\mu\prec\lambda
and
\mu\lessdot\rho

the distributions of
\nu
, with
|\nu/\lambda|=2
, in both situations I and II are equal, which is shown in Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
A.3
.
∎

7.
qRSt
for words

In the final section of this paper we show that restricting
qt{\rm RSK}^{*}
to
\{0,1\}
-matrices which have at most one
1
entry in each column yields a generalization of the RS correspondence for words to the Macdonald setting.

Let
\mu\subseteq\lambda
be two partitions and set
6
6

6

Due to the different definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}
in
[
1
]
the definition of
\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}
looks different, however it is easy to see that they coincide.

\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda/\mu}}\frac{b_{\lambda}(c)}{b_{\mu}(c)}.

For a semistandard Young tableau
T
define

\varphi_{T}(q,t)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\varphi_{T^{(i)}/T^{(i-1)}}(q,t).

It was shown by Macdonald
[
12
, Ch. VI (7.13)]
, that the Macdonald polynomials
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)
have the following monomial expansions

(58)

Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};q,t)=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}}\varphi_{T}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{T}.

Again, we take the perspective of viewing the above monomial expansion as the definition of the Macdonald polynomials
Q_{\lambda}
. The aim of
q{\rm RS}t
for words is a combinatorial proof of the squarefree part with respect to
\mathbf{y}
of the Cauchy identity (
19
) for Macdonald polynomials. As in the previous sections, we first derive a combinatorial proof for a certain commutation relation which is then used to define the
q{\rm RS}t
correspondence for words.

The
restricted
(q,t)
-down operator

D_{q,t}
is defined as

D_{q,t}\lambda=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\prec\lambda\\
|\lambda/\mu|=1\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\;\mu.

Theorem 7.1
.

The
(q,t)
-up and restricted
(q,t)
-down operators satisfy the commutation relation

(59)

D_{q,t}U_{x}(q,t)=U_{x}(q,t)D_{q,t}+\frac{1-t}{1-q}xU_{x}(q,t).

Proof.

Define the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(k,l)}(\lambda,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\mu\prec\lambda,\rho:|\lambda/\mu|=k,|\rho/\mu|=l\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{(k,l)}(\lambda,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\nu\succ\lambda,\rho:|\nu/\rho|=k,|\nu/\lambda|=l\},

together with the weights

\omega^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)=\psi_{\lambda/\mu}(q,t)\varphi_{\rho/\mu}(q,t)x^{|\lambda/\mu|},\qquad\overline{\omega}^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)=\psi_{\nu/\rho}(q,t)\varphi_{\nu/\lambda}(q,t)x^{|\nu/\rho|}.

Then equation (
59
) is equivalent to the system of equations

(60)

\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{(k+1,1)}(\lambda,\rho)}\overline{\omega}^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{(k+1,1)}(\lambda,\rho)}\omega^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)+\frac{1-t}{1-q}x\sum_{\mathcal{D}^{(k,0)}(\lambda,\rho)}\omega^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu),

for all partitions
\lambda,\rho
and nonnegative integers
k
.
Comparing the definitions of
\varphi
and
\varphi^{*}
we observe for two partitions
\mu\subseteq\rho
with
|\rho/\mu|\leq 1

\varphi_{\rho/\mu}=\begin{cases}\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}\quad&\mu=\rho,\\
\frac{1-t}{1-q}\varphi^{*}_{\rho/\mu}\quad&\mu\lessdot\rho,\end{cases}

where
\mu\lessdot\rho
is defined as
\mu\prec\rho
and
|\rho/\mu|=1
. This implies

\displaystyle\omega^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{(k,0)}(\lambda,\rho),

\displaystyle\omega^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-t}{1-q}\omega_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu)

\displaystyle\text{if }\mu\in\mathcal{D}^{(k+1,1)}(\lambda,\rho),

\displaystyle\overline{\omega}^{{\rm RS}}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-t}{1-q}\overline{\omega}_{\lambda,\rho}(\nu)

\displaystyle\text{if }\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{(k+1,1)}(\lambda,\rho),

By dividing (
60
) through
\frac{1-t}{1-q}
we obtain a special case of (
26
) which proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

q{\rm RS}t
for words defines a probabilistic bijection between the weighted set of
m\times n

\{0,1\}
-matrices with at most one entry equal to
1
in each column and weight
\omega
and
\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\lambda\subseteq(m^{n})}{\rm SSYT}(\lambda)\times{\rm SYT}^{\leq n}(\lambda)
and weight
\overline{\omega}^{{\rm RS}}
, where

\omega(A)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}(x_{i}y_{j})^{A_{i,j}}\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\overline{\omega}^{{\rm RS}}(P,Q)=\psi_{P}(q,t)\varphi_{Q}(q,t)\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}.

Moreover,
q{\rm RS}t
for words extends
q{\rm RS}t
as defined in
[
1
]
and specializes to the row insertion version of RS for words for
q,t\rightarrow 0
and the column insertion version of RS for words for
q,t\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

It follows from the proof of Theorem
7.1
, that
q{\rm RS}t
for words is the claimed probabilistic bijection.
Comparing with the growth rules in
[
1
, §4.6]
and the description of the probabilities in
[
1
, Proposition 4.5.1]
it is immediate that
q{\rm RS}t
for words is an extension of
q{\rm RS}t
as defined in
[
1
]
.
Finally the claim regarding the limits follows from Corollary
4.7
and the fact that
{\rm RSK}^{*}
restricted to
\{0,1\}
-matrices with at most one entry equal to
1
in each row yields RS for words.
∎

It is immediate, that we obtain the following insertion rules for
q{\rm RS}t
for words as a special case of Definition
4.15
.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
T
be a semistandard Young tableau, and
i
a positive integer. The
q{\rm RS}t
-insertion of
i
into
T
, denoted by

\widehat{T}=T\xleftarrow{qt{\rm RSK}^{*}}i,

is the probability distribution computed as follows.

•

For each
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{(k+1,1)}\left(T^{(i)},T^{(i-1)}\right)
, where
k=|T^{(i)}/T^{(i-1)}|
, place
i
in the cell
\nu/T^{(i)}
with probability
\mathcal{P}_{T^{(i)},T^{(i-1)}}\left(T^{(i-1)}\rightarrow\nu\right)
.

•

If an entry
z
was bumped during the process, reinsert it in the cell
\nu/T^{(z)}
with probability

\mathcal{P}_{T^{(z)},\widehat{T}^{(z-1)}}\left(T^{(z-1)}\rightarrow\nu\right)
, where
\nu\in\mathcal{U}^{(k,1)}\left(T^{(z)},\widehat{T}^{(z-1)}\right)
and
k=|T^{(z)}/T^{(z-1)}|
.

Appendix A
Two technical lemmas for the Jack limit

Lemma A.1
.

For partitions
\mu\lessdot\rho\prec\nu
and
\mu\prec\lambda\subseteq\nu
satisfying
\lambda\cap\rho=\mu
and
|\nu/\lambda|=2
the probability to obtain
\nu
in the partial growth diagrams I and II are equal.

\lambda

Proof.

Let
d
be the number of removable inner corners of
\mu
with respect to
(\lambda,\mu)
and
R_{i},I_{i},S_{j},O_{j}
for
i\in[d]
and
j\in[0,d]
be the points defined for
\lambda,\mu
. Since we regard removable and addable corners for various partitions at the same time, we identify in the following each outer and inner corner with their “index” and can therefore omit referring to the shapes they are defined on. The removable inner corners of
\mu
with respect to
(\lambda,\mu)
have indices in
[d]
and the addable outer corners of
\lambda
have indices in
[0,d]
.
Further we use the notation

p\bigl(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{c},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{S}^{c}\bigr):=\prod_{s\in\mathbf{S}}\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}}(S_{s}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}^{c}}(S_{s}-O_{j})}\prod_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\in\mathbf{S}^{c}}(R_{r}-O_{j})}{\prod\limits_{i\in\mathbf{R}^{c}}(R_{r}-I_{i})}.

We regard the following two cases.

Case 1:
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=2
.

There exist two addable outer corners
s_{1},s_{2}\in[0,d]
such that
\rho
is obtained by adding
s_{1}
to
\mu
and
\nu
is obtained by adding both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
to
\lambda
, see Figure
12
for a sketch. Indeed, the only possible partition for
*
in situation I is the partition
\lambda\cup\rho
which implies that
(\lambda\cup\rho)/\lambda=\rho/\mu
is an addable cell
s_{1}\in[0,d]
. The condition
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=2
implies the existence of a partition
\lambda_{2}\neq\lambda\cup\rho
which satisfies
\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu
. By definition
\lambda_{2}
is therefore obtained by adding an outer corner
s_{2}\neq s_{1}
to
\lambda
. The conditions of the right square in the growth diagram II imply
\nu=\lambda_{2}\cup\rho
which yields the above claim.

The possible local configurations for I and II are shown next.

Figure 12
.
A sketch for Lemma
A.1
.

The points corresponding to the newly defined corners satisfy

R_{x}=S_{s_{1}},\quad I_{x}=\frac{t}{q}S_{s_{1}},\quad S_{x^{N}}=O_{x^{N}}=tS_{s_{1}},\quad S_{x^{W}}=O_{x^{W}}=q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}.

Hence we obtain for the probability of
\nu
in situation I the expression

(61)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda\cup\rho,\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d]\cup\{x\},\{s_{2}\},\{x^{N},x^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(S_{s_{2}}-\frac{t}{q}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{(S_{s_{2}}-tS_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}.

On the other hand, the probability to obtain
\nu
in situation IIa or IIb is

(62)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda\cup\rho)\mathcal{P}_{\lambda\cup\rho,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)+\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{2})\\
=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{2}\},\{x^{N},x^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
+p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{2}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}\\
\times\frac{(S_{s_{1}}-tS_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{1}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{2}}-tS_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}\\
+\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}\left(1-\frac{(S_{s_{1}}-tS_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{1}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})}{(S_{s_{2}}-tS_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})}\right).

By taking the fraction of (
61
) and (
62
) we obtain

(63)

\frac{tS_{s_{1}}-qS_{s_{2}}}{S_{s_{1}}(1-q+qt)-qS_{s_{2}}},

which yields
1
in the Jack limit and hence proves the assertion for this case.

Case 2:
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=1
.

The fact that
\lambda\cup\rho
is the only partition
\pi
satisfying
\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu
implies that
\nu
is obtained by adding either the outer corner
x^{N}
or
x^{W}
to
\lambda\cup\rho
. In both cases the local configurations for I and II are shown next.
\mu
\rho
\rho
\lambda
\lambda\cup\rho
\nu
X
I
\mu
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\lambda\cup\rho
\nu
X
II

If
\nu
is obtained by adding the cell
x^{N}
to
\lambda\cup\rho
, the probability to obtain
\nu
in situation I is

(64)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda\cup\rho,\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d]\cup\{x\},\{x^{N}\},\{x^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(S_{x^{N}}-\frac{t}{q}S_{1})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{x^{N}}-I_{i})}{(S_{x^{N}}-S_{x^{W}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{x^{N}}-O_{j})},

and in situation II

(65)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda\cup\rho)\mathcal{P}_{\lambda\cup\rho,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{x^{N}\},\{x^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}\cdot\frac{(S_{s_{1}}-O_{x^{W}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{x^{N}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}{(S_{x^{N}}-O_{x^{W}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{x^{N}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}\\
=\frac{(S_{s_{1}}-O_{x^{W}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{x^{N}}-I_{i})}{(S_{x^{N}}-O_{x^{W}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{x^{N}}-O_{j})}.

If
\nu
is however obtained by adding
x^{W}
to
\lambda\cup\rho
, we obtain the corresponding probabilities by interchanging
x^{N}
and
x^{W}
in (
64
) and (
65
).

Dividing (
64
) by (
65
), we obtain after cancellation
t
or, when replacing first
x^{N}
and
x^{W}
,
q^{-1}
. Taking the Jack limit in both cases yields
1
and hence finishes the proof.
∎

Remark A.2

(The non-generic case)
.

Assume in the setting of Case 1 of the above proof that
x^{N}
is not an addable cell of
(\lambda\cup\rho)
, i.e., we are not in the generic case. This implies that there exists an
i_{0}
with
S_{x^{N}}=I_{i_{0}}
such that
i_{0}
is not a removable cell of
\rho
.
In this case the probability of
\nu
in I is given by

(66)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda\cup\rho,\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)=p\Bigl(\emptyset,\{x\}\cup[d]\setminus\{i_{0}\},\{s_{2}\},\{x^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(S_{s_{2}}-\frac{t}{q}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]\setminus\{i_{0}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}.

The right hand sides of (
61
) and (
66
) differ by the factor

\frac{S_{s_{2}}-I_{i_{0}}}{S_{s_{2}}-tS_{s_{1}}}=1,

where the equality follows from
S_{x^{N}}=tS_{s_{1}}
.
This observation can be generalized as follows. For two sets
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{c}
and
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{c}
holds

(67)

\frac{p\bigl(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{c}\setminus A,\mathbf{S},\mathbf{S}^{c}\setminus B\bigr)}{p\bigl(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{c},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{S}^{c}\bigr)}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbf{S}\\
1\leq i\leq n\end{subarray}}\frac{S_{s}-O_{b_{i}}}{S_{s}-I_{a_{i}}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\in\mathbf{R}\\
1\leq i\leq n\end{subarray}}\frac{R_{r}-I_{a_{i}}}{R_{r}-O_{b_{i}}}.

Comparing to the generic case, a cell which is an outer corner in the generic case with index
b_{i}
becomes not addable if it lies directly north and west of a cell which is an inner corner in the generic case with index
a_{i}
, compare to Figure
13
. This implies the identity
O_{b_{i}}=I_{a_{i}}
.
By choosing
A
to be the set of inner cells of the generic case, which are not removable and
B
as the set of outer corners of the generic case which are not addable, the expression in (
67
) is equal to
1
. This implies that we may assume the generic case for all calculations.

a
b
O_{b}
I_{a}
a
b
O_{b}
I_{a}

Figure 13
.
For the “generic case” on the left the cell
b
is addable and the cell
a
is removable. The cell
b
stops being addable and the cell
a
stops being removable once the row lengths of both rows are equal as shown on the right. In this case we obtain the identity
O_{b}=I_{a}
.

Lemma A.3
.

For partitions
\mu\lessdot\rho\prec\lambda\subseteq\nu
satisfying
|\nu/\lambda|=2
the probability to obtain
\nu
in the partial growth diagrams I and II are equal.
\mu
\rho
\rho
\lambda
*
\nu
X
I
\mu
\mu
\rho
\lambda
*
\nu
X
II

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
A.1
let
d
be the number of removable inner corners of
\mu
with respect to
(\lambda,\mu)
and define the points
R_{i},I_{i},S_{j}
and
O_{j}
for
\lambda,\mu
. We regard three different cases where we assume
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=2
for Case 1 and 2, and
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=1
in Case 3.

Case 1.

In this case we assume
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=2
and that
\nu
is obtained by adding two outer corners with indices
s_{1},s_{2}\in[0,d]
to
\lambda
. We denote by
\lambda_{i}
the partition obtained by adding
s_{i}
to
\lambda
for
i=1,2
. See Figure
14
for a sketch.

s_{2}

S_{x^{N}}
S_{s_{2}^{W}}
R_{x}

Figure 14
.
A sketch for Lemma
A.1
.

The possible local configurations for I and II are shown next.
\mu

\rho
\lambda
\lambda_{1}
\nu
X
Ia
\mu
\rho
\rho
\lambda
\lambda_{2}
\nu
X
Ib
\mu
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\lambda_{1}
\nu
X
IIa
\mu
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\lambda_{2}
\nu
X
IIb

Denote by
x
the inner cell
\rho/\mu
, by
x^{N}
the cell directly above
x
and by
s_{i}^{W}
the cell on the left of the cell
s_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
The according points for these cells satisfy

I_{x}=\frac{t}{q}R_{x},\qquad S_{x^{N}}=O_{x^{N}}=tR_{x},\qquad S_{s_{i}^{W}}=O_{s_{i}^{W}}=q^{-1}S_{s_{i}},

for
i=1,2
. Similar to Remark
A.2
we can assume that
x^{N}
and
s_{i}^{W}
are addable outer corners of
\lambda_{i}
with respect to
(\lambda_{i},\rho)
.

The probability to obtain
\nu
in Ia is

(68)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{1}\},\{x^{N}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
\times p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d]\cup\{x\},\{s_{2}\},\{x^{N}s_{1}^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}\\
\times\frac{(S_{s_{2}}-\frac{t}{q}R_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{(S_{s_{2}}-tR_{x})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}\\
=F\frac{(R_{x}-O_{s_{2}})(S_{s_{2}}-\frac{t}{q}R_{x})}{(S_{s_{1}}-O_{s_{2}})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})},

where

F=\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})}{(S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})(S_{s_{2}}-tR_{x})}\prod_{i\in[d]}\frac{(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})}{(R_{x}-I_{i})}\\
\times\prod_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}\frac{(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})}.

We obtain the corresponding probability of
\nu
in configuration Ib by interchanging
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
in (
68
).

The probability of
\nu
in configuration IIa is given by

(69)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{2}\},\{x^{N},s_{1}^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}\\
\times\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})(R_{x}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{2}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{2}}-tR_{x})(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}(S_{s_{2}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}\\
=F\cdot\frac{(R_{x}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})}{(S_{s_{2}}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})(S_{s_{1}}-S_{s_{2}})}.

In order to obtain the probability for
\nu
in IIb, we interchange
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
in (
69
).

Dividing the probability of obtaining
\nu
in I by the probability of obtaining
\nu
in II yields

\frac{1}{q}+\frac{(1-q)(qS_{s_{1}}-S_{s_{2}})(qS_{s_{2}}-S_{s_{1}})}{q\left((1+q)(R_{x}^{2}qt-S_{s_{1}}S_{s_{2}})+q(S_{s_{1}}+S_{s_{2}})(S_{s_{1}}+S_{s_{2}}-(q+t)R_{x})\right)}.

It is not difficult to check, that the second fraction in the above expression has Jack limit
0
, which proves this case.

Case 2.

In this case we assume again
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=2
, but
\nu
is now obtained by adding an outer corner with index
s_{1}\in[0,d]
and
x^{N}
to
\lambda
. Denote by
\lambda_{1}
(resp.,
\lambda^{N}
) the partition obtained by adding
s_{1}
(resp.,
x^{N}
) to
\lambda
. The possible local configurations are shown next.

\mu

The probability to obtain
\nu
is in situation Ia

(70)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{1}\},\{x^{N}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d]\cup\{x\},\{x^{N}\},\{s_{1}^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}\\
\times\frac{(tR_{x}-\frac{t}{q}R_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(tR_{x}-I_{i})}{(tR_{x}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(tR_{x}-O_{j})},

in situation Ib

(71)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda^{N})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda^{N},\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{x^{N}\},[0,d]\Bigr)p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(tR_{x}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]}(tR_{x}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})},

and in situation II

(72)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{x^{N}\},\{s_{1}^{W}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}\cdot\frac{(R_{x}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(tR_{x}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(tR_{x}-q^{-1}S_{s_{1}})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(tR_{x}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}.

By adding the probabilities of
\nu
in Ia and Ib and dividing by the probability of
\nu
in II, we obtain

1+\frac{(1-q)(1-t)}{q(1-\frac{S_{s_{1}}}{qR_{x}})},

which yields
1
in the Jack limit and hence proves the claim in this case.

Case 3.

In the last case we assume
|\{\pi:\lambda\lessdot\pi\lessdot\nu\}|=1
. This implies that there exists an outer corner
s_{1}\in[0,d]
such that
\nu
is obtained by adding
s_{1}
and
s_{1}^{W}
to

. Denote again by
\lambda_{1}
the partition obtained by adding
s_{1}
to
\lambda
.
The two possible local configurations are shown next.
\mu

\lambda_{1}
\nu

I
\mu
\mu
\rho
\lambda
\lambda_{1}
\nu

The probability of
\nu
in situation I is

(73)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\rho\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{1}\},\{x^{N}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d]\cup\{x\},\{s_{1}^{W}\},\{x^{N}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}\\
\times\frac{(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-\frac{t}{q}R_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})},

(74)

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda,\mu}(\mu\rightarrow\lambda_{1})\mathcal{P}_{\lambda_{1},\rho}(\mu\rightarrow\nu)\\
=p\Bigl(\emptyset,[d],\{s_{1}\},[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)p\Bigl(\{x\},[d],\{s_{1}^{W}\},\{x^{N}\}\cup[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}\Bigr)\\
=\frac{\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})}{\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})}\\
\times\frac{(R_{x}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-I_{i})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(R_{x}-O_{j})}{(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-tR_{x})\prod\limits_{j\in[0,d]\setminus\{s_{1}\}}(q^{-1}S_{s_{1}}-O_{j})\prod\limits_{i\in[d]}(R_{x}-I_{i})}.

By dividing (
73
) through (
74
) we obtain
\frac{1}{q}
which yields
1
in the Jack limit.
∎
